## 原稿 5: line1_yamabe_endpoint.md

来源：`mathematics/current/line1_yamabe_endpoint.md`；范围：`full`。

原文件 SHA-256：`62545dd028fbd09dc5e3d32388ac56f2f4019c30b90ccc3611b3557a65fbcead`。

# What negative Yamabe normalization preserves—and what it does not

23 September 2026. Pointwise almost-Einstein input admits a uniformly small
scalar-normalizing conformal change in \(C^1\) and normalized \(W^{2,2}\),
without injectivity-radius, Sobolev, or scalar spectral-gap assumptions.
Nevertheless that conformal change need not preserve pointwise Ricci
approximation: the normalized Ricci error can diverge. An explicit
counterexample below occurs on a fixed product of hyperbolic surfaces,
with bounded input sectional curvature and no collapse.

Throughout, \(\Delta=d^*d=-\operatorname{div}\nabla\), and all norms of
\(u\) and its derivatives use the original metric unless indicated.

## 1. A uniform positive result

**Theorem 1.** Let \((M^4,g)\) be closed, connected, and smooth, and suppose
\[
 \|g^{-1}\operatorname{Ric}_g+I\|_{\infty,\mathrm{op}}
 \le\varepsilon\le\frac14.
 \tag{1}
\]
There is a unique positive smooth function \(u\) for which
\(\widehat g=u^2g\) has scalar curvature \(-4\). Put \(e=s_g+4\),
\(V=\operatorname{Vol}(g)\). Then
\[
 1-\varepsilon\le u^2\le1+\varepsilon,
 \qquad \|du\|_\infty\le4\varepsilon,
 \tag{2}
\]
In particular \(\|g^{-1}\widehat g-I\|_\infty\le\varepsilon\) and
\((1-\varepsilon)^2V\le\operatorname{Vol}(\widehat g)
\le(1+\varepsilon)^2V\).
\[
 \int\bigl((u-1)^2+|du|^2+|\nabla^2u|^2\bigr)
 \le\frac38\int e^2\le6\varepsilon^2V.
 \tag{3}
\]
The normalized metric has the integral Einstein-error bound
\[
 \int|\operatorname{Ric}_{\widehat g}+\widehat g|^2_{\widehat g}
 \le\int|\operatorname{Ric}^{\circ}_g|^2_g
 \le4\varepsilon^2V,
 \qquad
 \fint_{\widehat g}|\operatorname{Ric}_{\widehat g}+\widehat g|^2
 \le\frac{4\varepsilon^2}{(1-\varepsilon)^2}.
 \tag{4}
\]
Thus the scalar-normalizing change is \(C^1\)-small, with a second-order
integral estimate. No bound on \(\nabla\operatorname{Ric}\) or full
curvature has been assumed. No monopole assumption is needed.

### Proof of existence, amplitude, and integral estimates

Since \(s_g\le-3\), the conformal Yamabe constant is negative. The
Yamabe theorem gives a negative constant-scalar representative, which can
be uniquely rescaled to scalar curvature \(-4\). Uniqueness also follows
directly: the ratio of two such factors solves
\(6\Delta z-4z+4z^3=0\) on one normalized metric; the maximum and
minimum principles force \(z=1\).

The factor satisfies
\[
 6\Delta u+(-4+e)u+4u^3=0.
 \tag{5}
\]
At its maximum and minimum the signs of \(\Delta u\) give (2)'s
amplitude bound. Write \(v=u-1\) and \(a=4u(u+1)\). Then
\[
 6\Delta v+av=-eu,\qquad
 6\le a\le10,\qquad u\le9/8.
 \tag{6}
\]
Pairing with \(v\) gives
\[
 \|v\|_2\le\frac3{16}\|e\|_2,
 \quad \|dv\|_2^2\le\frac9{256}\|e\|_2^2,
 \quad \|\Delta v\|_2\le\frac12\|e\|_2.
 \tag{7}
\]
The last bound follows by substituting the first one into (6).
Integrated Bochner and \(\operatorname{Ric}\ge-5g/4\) give
\[
 \|\nabla^2v\|_2^2
 =\|\Delta v\|_2^2-\int\operatorname{Ric}(dv,dv)
 \le\frac{301}{1024}\|e\|_2^2.
 \tag{8}
\]
Adding the three bounds proves (3). These are estimates on the original
closed metric, not elliptic estimates in uniformly controlled charts.

### Proof of the maximum gradient bound

Rewrite (5) as
\[
 (\Delta+4/3)v=F,
 \qquad F=-eu/6-2v^2-(2/3)v^3,
 \qquad \|F\|_\infty\le2\varepsilon.
 \tag{9}
\]
Here \(|v|\le\varepsilon\) and \(|e|\le4\varepsilon\).
For the heat semigroup \(P_t=e^{-t\Delta}\), Bochner under
\(\operatorname{Ric}\ge-Kg\) gives
\[
 |\nabla P_t f|^2
 \le\frac{K}{1-e^{-2Kt}}
       \bigl(P_t(f^2)-(P_tf)^2\bigr)
 \le\left(K+\frac1{2t}\right)\|f\|_\infty^2.
 \tag{10}
\]
One direct derivation is to differentiate
\(P_s[(P_{t-s}f)^2]\), integrate from \(0\) to \(t\), and use
\(|\nabla P_t f|^2\le e^{2Ks}P_s|\nabla P_{t-s}f|^2\).
The second inequality uses \(1-e^{-x}\ge x/(1+x)\).
The positive-mass resolvent therefore satisfies
\[
 \|\nabla(\Delta+m)^{-1}f\|_\infty
 \le\left(\frac{\sqrt K}{m}+\sqrt{\frac\pi{2m}}\right)\|f\|_\infty.
 \tag{11}
\]
Indeed, integrate \(e^{-mt}\nabla P_tf\) over \(t>0\); the bound
is integrable both at zero and at infinity. With \(K=5/4\) and
\(m=4/3\), the coefficient is
\(3\sqrt5/8+\sqrt{3\pi/8}<2\). Equations (9)–(11) prove (2).

### Proof of the Ricci-energy bound

For a negative Yamabe class, \(\int s_{\widehat g}^2\le\int s_g^2\).
For example, if \(\widehat g_0=z^2g\) has volume one, its Yamabe
constant is
\(Y=\int(6|dz|^2+s_gz^2)\ge-\|s_-\|_2\); hence
\(\int s_{\widehat g}^2=Y^2\le\int s_g^2\).
In four dimensions, \(\int|W^+|^2\) is conformally invariant and
\[
 \int|\operatorname{Ric}^{\circ}|^2
 =4\int|W^+|^2+\frac1{12}\int s^2-8\pi^2(2\chi+3\tau).
 \tag{12}
\]
This is the conformal monotonicity proved in
[`line1_energy_selection.md`](line1_energy_selection.md), Section 4;
the argument is included here to make its lack of monopole assumptions
explicit. Thus the trace-free Ricci energy cannot increase. Since
\(s_{\widehat g}=-4\), this is its full Einstein-error energy.
Finally \(\operatorname{Vol}(\widehat g)\ge(1-\varepsilon)^2V\).
This proves (4). All integrals and averages on the left side of (4)
use \(d\mu_{\widehat g}\), not the input volume measure.
For a nonorientable manifold, apply the curvature-energy argument on its
oriented double cover and divide the integrals by two; uniqueness makes
the scalar-normalizing factor descend. The same estimates therefore hold.

## 2. An actual failure of pointwise Ricci preservation

**Theorem 2.** There is a fixed closed manifold
\(M=\Sigma_1\times\Sigma_2\), with both genera at least two, and
smooth metrics \(g_L\) such that
\[
 \|g_L^{-1}\operatorname{Ric}_{g_L}+I\|_\infty
       =O((\log L)/L)\to0,
 \tag{13}
\]
while their scalar-\(-4\) Yamabe representatives satisfy
\[
 \liminf_{L\to\infty}\frac1{\log L}
 \|\widehat g_L^{-1}\operatorname{Ric}_{\widehat g_L}+I\|_\infty
 \ge\frac23.
 \tag{14}
\]
In particular, the normalized maximum Ricci error tends to infinity.
The input metrics have uniformly bounded sectional curvature, diameter,
and positive injectivity radius and scalar spectral gap. They converge
in \(C^{1,\alpha}\), for every fixed \(0<\alpha<1\), to a fixed
negative Einstein product. Thus none of those geometric controls repairs
the failed endpoint conclusion.

### A smooth two-dimensional bump

Fix hyperbolic metrics \(h_i\) of curvature \(-1\). In an isothermal
coordinate disk about \(p\in\Sigma_1\), choose coordinates with
\(h_1(p)=dx^2+dy^2\). Take a fixed radial smooth cutoff \(\chi\),
equal to one near \(p=(0,0)\), supported inside the disk. Put
\[
 v_L(x,y)=-\frac{\log L}{2L}\,\chi(x,y)\,xy
                 \log(x^2+y^2+e^{-2L}),
 \tag{15}
\]
for \(L>e\), and extend by zero. With \(r^2=x^2+y^2\) and \(b>0\), direct
differentiation gives
\[
 (\partial_x^2+\partial_y^2)
     [xy\log(r^2+b)]
 =4xy\frac{2r^2+3b}{(r^2+b)^2}.
 \tag{16}
\]
Its absolute value is at most four. The function and its first
derivatives are bounded uniformly for \(b=e^{-2L}\); cutoff errors
have fixed support away from the singular point. In two dimensions the
isothermal Laplacian is a smooth fixed multiple of the Euclidean one.
Consequently
\[
 \|v_L\|_{C^1}+\|\Delta_{h_1}v_L\|_\infty=O((\log L)/L),
 \quad \|\nabla_{h_1}^2v_L\|_\infty\le C\log L,
 \quad \nabla_{h_1}^2v_L(p)=(\log L)H,
 \tag{17}
\]
where \(H=dx\otimes dy+dy\otimes dx\). At \(p\), \(dv_L=0\),
so no connection term changes this Hessian. Interpolation gives
\[
 [dv_L]_{C^{0,\alpha}}
 \le C_\alpha\|dv_L\|_\infty^{1-\alpha}
                    \|\nabla^2v_L\|_\infty^\alpha
 \le C_\alpha(\log L)L^{\alpha-1}\longrightarrow0
\]
for every fixed \(\alpha<1\). Thus \(v_L\to0\) in
\(C^{1,\alpha}\) despite the unbounded central Hessian.

### The input Ricci tensor

Set
\[
 g_L=e^{2v_L}h_1+h_2.
 \tag{18}
\]
The first factor's Gaussian curvature is
\(K_L=e^{-2v_L}(-1+\Delta_{h_1}v_L)\); the second has curvature
\(-1\). Thus (17) proves (13). All mixed sectional curvatures vanish,
and the two factor curvatures remain in a fixed negative interval.
This gives uniform sectional-curvature bounds. Bilipschitz convergence
to the fixed product bounds diameter and the scalar spectral gap.
There are no conjugate points on either factor, and lengths of
noncontractible loops are bounded below by bilipschitz comparison;
the product therefore also has a uniform positive injectivity radius.

### Exact scalar normalization exposes the Hessian

Seek a positive scalar-normalizing factor depending only on \(\Sigma_1\).
Its equation, after multiplication by \(e^{2v}\), is
\[
 6\Delta_{h_1}u+(-2+2\Delta_{h_1}v-2e^{2v})u+4e^{2v}u^3=0.
 \tag{19}
\]
The substitution \(u=e^{-v/3}w\) cancels the entire \(\Delta v\)
term and gives
\[
 6\Delta_{h_1}w+4\langle dv,dw\rangle
  +\left(-\frac23|dv|^2-2-2e^{2v}\right)w
  +4e^{4v/3}w^3=0.
 \tag{20}
\]
For fixed \(0<\alpha<1\), the left side is a smooth map from
\(C^{1,\alpha}(\Sigma_1)\times C^{2,\alpha}(\Sigma_1)\) to
\(C^{0,\alpha}(\Sigma_1)\). At \((v,w)=(0,1)\), its derivative
in \(w\) is \(6\Delta_{h_1}+8\), an isomorphism. The implicit
function theorem supplies positive \(w_L\to1\) in \(C^{2,\alpha}\).
Then \(u_L=e^{-v_L/3}w_L\) solves the actual four-dimensional scalar
equation. Uniqueness in Theorem 1 identifies it with the negative
Yamabe factor; no symmetry of a hypothetical factor was assumed.

At \(p\), (17) now gives
\[
 u_L(p)=w_L(p)\to1,\quad du_L(p)=dw_L(p)\to0,\quad
 \nabla_{g_L}^2u_L(p)
 =\nabla_{h_1}^2w_L(p)-\frac{w_L(p)\log L}{3}H.
 \tag{21}
\]
Here the connections of \(g_L\) and the fixed product converge in
\(C^0\), and \(dv_L(p)=0\). The four-dimensional conformal formula
is
\[
 \operatorname{Ric}^{\circ}_{u^2g}
 =\operatorname{Ric}^{\circ}_g
       -2u^{-1}(\nabla_g^2u)^\circ
       +4u^{-2}(du\otimes du)^\circ.
 \tag{22}
\]
The trace-free operation on covariant two-tensors is unchanged by a
conformal rescaling. At \(p\), the background connection is precisely
the fixed product connection because \(v_L(p)=dv_L(p)=0\). Also
\(\Delta v_L(p)=0\), so the input Ricci tensor is exactly
\(-g_L\) there. The first term on the right of (21), extended by zero
on the second factor, tends to zero. Substitution into (22) therefore
gives the covariant-tensor expansion
\[
 (\operatorname{Ric}_{\widehat g_L}+\widehat g_L)(p,y)
       =\frac23(\log L)H+o(1).
\]
The coefficient \(w_L(p)\) cancels against \(u_L(p)^{-1}\).
Raising an index uses \(\widehat g_L(p,y)=w_L(p)^2g_0(p,y)\).
Since \(w_L(p)\to1\) and \(\|H\|_{\rm op}=1\), the pointwise
operator norm divided by \(\log L\) tends to \(2/3\). This proves
(14). If the prefactor \(\log L\) is replaced by one throughout,
the same argument gives the finite endpoint defect \(2/3\); the
slowly growing prefactor establishes the stronger unbounded statement.

## 3. Consequence for the first research line

The counterexample is compatible with all integral results above:
\(\|\nabla^2u_L\|_2\to0\), even though its value at \(p\)
diverges. Each input is a Kähler product on a fixed general-type
surface. For its canonical class the exact Kähler identities give
\(D_{LB}=\int(s-\bar s)^2/(64\pi^2)\to0\) and
\(p-a^2\to0\). Thus the obstruction is not missing monopole data,
negative-square control, or a scalar spectral gap.

This rules out one proposed passage from pointwise input to a convenient
scalar-normalized pointwise input. It does not rule out other corrections:
the limiting product itself is Einstein. The theorem also does not show
that actual weighted-monopole forms must have zeros; that is a different
question and requires its own argument.

Round 14 normalized a specific shear family whose lifted coefficients
had uniform higher-derivative bounds, sufficient for Schauder estimates.
Theorem 1 needs no such bounds, and Theorem 2 explains why its universal
conclusion must stop short of a maximum-norm Hessian or Ricci estimate.
The logarithmic bump is the classical endpoint Laplacian/Hessian
mechanism; the contribution here is its realization inside an actual
Ricci-pinched family and its exact scalar-normalizing equation.

`line1_yamabe_endpoint_check.py` verifies finite differential identities,
norm conventions, and constants. Existence uses the classical negative
Yamabe theorem and the explicitly stated fixed-background implicit
function theorem, not the finite checks.



---

## 原稿 6: line1_conformal_neck.md

来源：`mathematics/current/line1_conformal_neck.md`；范围：`full`。

原文件 SHA-256：`6234623a6d9df672acdbf765cfb92423516cfe234858cbe092303344ab661d8a`。

# A conformal-neck counterexample to scalar stability without connectivity

23 September 2026. This note proves a concrete limitation of the first-line
functional-stability program. It first records a valid Ricci/connection
bridge, then constructs metrics for which every energy on its right tends
to zero while scalar variance and relative Einstein defect do not. All
inputs are classical. No literature-priority claim is made.

The example stays on one fixed smooth manifold which already admits
negative Einstein metrics. It is not a counterexample to Yau26.

## 1. The valid non-Kähler bridge, and the missing conclusion

Use the curvature conventions of [Round 17](round17_integral.md) and the
[scalar-band note](line1_scalar_band_remainder.md). Let an actual ordinary
Seiberg–Witten solution have real determinant curvature \(\mathcal F\), with
\([\mathcal F/(2\pi)]=a\). Write

\[
 p=(a_g^+)^2>0,\quad T=2\chi+3\tau,\quad
 P=\int|\operatorname{Ric}^{\circ}|^2,\quad
 B_p=\int s^2-32\pi^2p,
\]
\[
 D=\frac1{4\pi^2}\int\left(\frac{s^2}{24}
                +\frac{|W^+|^2}{2}\right)-\frac p2.
\]

Ordinary SW gives \(B_p\ge0\). Indeed, with \(\rho=|\Phi|^2\), the exact
Round-17 identity refines to

\[
 \boxed{B_p=\|s+\rho\|_2^2+8\|\nabla_A\Phi\|_2^2
 +8\left(\|\mathcal F^-\|_2^2-4\pi^2(p-a^2)\right).}
 \tag{1}
\]

The last parenthesis is nonnegative by Hodge orthogonality. If
\(\mathcal F_H\) is the harmonic representative of \(2\pi a\), exactness of
\(\mathcal F-\mathcal F_H\) equates the squared norms of its two Hodge
components, so (1) also proves

\[
 \|\mathcal F-\mathcal F_H\|_2^2\le B_p/4,
 \qquad \|\nabla_A\Phi\|_2^2\le B_p/8.
 \tag{2}
\]

These are controls of actual determinant curvature and an actual spinor,
not a formal cohomology substitution. They do not assert control of the
connection one-form modulo gauge without an additional elliptic estimate.

Chern–Weil gives the exact non-Kähler identity

\[
 \boxed{P+\frac{B_p}{4}
 =8\pi^2\bigl((p-a^2)+(a^2-T)\bigr)+32\pi^2D.}
 \tag{3}
\]

Consequently small LB defect, small period excess, and small topological
cost really do bound trace-free Ricci energy. If \(a^2\ge T\), all three
terms on the right are nonnegative. This elementary classical consequence
does not, however, bound scalar variance.

There is even a scalar-band estimate for \(B_p\), independent of any
Poincaré constant. Assume \(-S\le s\le-s_0<0\), and the conformal-class SW
solvability used in the scalar-band note. Put \(w=\sqrt6|W^+|\) and
\(t=s+2w\). Its retained defect split gives
\(\int t^2\le288\pi^2D\). Direct algebra gives

\[
 B_p=64\pi^2D+\frac23\int st-\frac13\int t^2,
\]
\[
 \boxed{0\le B_p\le64\pi^2D+8\sqrt2\pi S\sqrt{VD}.}
 \tag{4}
\]

Thus a fixed scalar band and \(D/V\to0\) also control the normalized
energies in (1)–(2). The following example satisfies stronger hypotheses:
\(p=a^2=T\) exactly, and its ordinary scalar defect tends to zero. Scalar
variance nevertheless remains positive.

## 2. Fixed smooth topology and a separating hyperbolic collar

Fix integers \(g_-,g_+\ge1\), \(g=g_-+g_+\), and \(h\ge2\). Set

\[
 M=\Sigma_g\times\Sigma_h,
 \qquad A=8(g-1)(h-1)=2\chi(M)+3\tau(M)>0.
\]

Choose a separating curve in \(\Sigma_g\) whose two sides have genera
\(g_-\) and \(g_+\). Hyperbolic pairs of pants, equivalently
Fenchel–Nielsen coordinates, give curvature \(-1\) metrics \(b_\ell\) on
this fixed marked surface for which this curve is a geodesic of length
\(\ell\downarrow0\). Fix a curvature \(-1\) metric \(h_F\) on \(\Sigma_h\),
and put

\[
 g_\ell=b_\ell\oplus h_F.
\]

Then \(\operatorname{Ric}_{g_\ell}=-g_\ell\), \(s_{g_\ell}=-4\), and

\[
 V_0=\operatorname{Vol}(g_\ell)
 =16\pi^2(g-1)(h-1)=2\pi^2A.
 \tag{5}
\]

The separating geodesic has an embedded collar

\[
 (-w_\ell,w_\ell)\times(\mathbb R/\mathbb Z),\qquad
 b_\ell=dt^2+\ell^2\cosh^2t\,d\theta^2,
 \quad w_\ell=\operatorname{arsinh}\frac1{\sinh(\ell/2)}\to\infty.
 \tag{6}
\]

This is the classical collar lemma; a primary research reference recalling
it is [Rupflin–Topping, Appendix A, Lemma A.1](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1605.06691).
Only (6), not a convergence theorem for moduli spaces, is needed below.

Cutting at \(t=0\) gives geodesic-boundary hyperbolic surfaces of areas
\(2\pi(2g_\pm-1)\), by Gauss–Bonnet. The corresponding product-side volumes
are therefore exactly

\[
 V_\pm=8\pi^2(2g_\pm-1)(h-1)>0,\qquad V_-+V_+=V_0.
 \tag{7}
\]

## 3. A fixed-width transition and a uniform negative scalar band

Fix unequal positive constants \(c_-,c_+\). Choose a smooth function
\(f:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R\) which equals \(\log c_-\) for \(t\le-R\)
and \(\log c_+\) for \(t\ge R\), and is constant on neighborhoods of those
end intervals. Here \(R<\infty\) is fixed. It can be chosen large enough
that

\[
 6\bigl(\|f''\|_\infty+\|f'\|_\infty+\|f'\|_\infty^2\bigr)\le2.
 \tag{8}
\]

Alternatively fix \(R\) first and choose \(\log(c_+/c_-)\ne0\) sufficiently
small. For \(w_\ell>R\), extend \(f(t)\) to the two complementary sides by
its respective constants; call the resulting smooth function \(f_\ell\)
on \(M\), constant in the fiber direction. Set

\[
 \widehat g_\ell=e^{2f_\ell}g_\ell.
 \tag{9}
\]

Use the Laplacian \(\Delta=\operatorname{div}\nabla\), with nonpositive
spectrum. On the transition region,

\[
 |df_\ell|^2=(f')^2,\qquad
 \Delta f_\ell=f''+\tanh(t)f',\qquad
 \widehat s_\ell=e^{-2f_\ell}
       \left[-4-6\bigl(\Delta f_\ell+|df_\ell|^2\bigr)\right].
 \tag{10}
\]

Writing \(f_{\min},f_{\max}\) for the fixed extrema, (8) implies

\[
 -6e^{-2f_{\min}}\le\widehat s_\ell
 \le-2e^{-2f_{\max}}<0.
 \tag{11}
\]

The support of the derivatives lies in
\(N_\ell=[-R,R]\times S^1\times\Sigma_h\), whose unscaled volume is

\[
 \operatorname{Vol}_{g_\ell}(N_\ell)
 =2\ell\sinh R\,\operatorname{Area}(h_F)=O(\ell).
 \tag{12}
\]

All derivatives of \(f_\ell\) of each fixed order are uniformly bounded:
in the collar frame the coefficients are built from bounded derivatives
of \(\tanh t\) and the fixed function \(f\). Hence the full curvature and
each fixed covariant derivative of curvature of \(\widehat g_\ell\) are
uniformly bounded as well. Volumes are bounded above and away from zero.
Injectivity radius and global connectivity are not uniformly controlled.

## 4. Actual monopole class, exact periods, and exact energy identities

The product almost-complex structures determined by the two oriented
surface metrics are homotopic as \(\ell\) varies. Choose the canonical or
conjugate canonical Spin\(^c\) structure, and write \(a=\pm c_1(K_M)\) for
its real first Chern class. This is one fixed class and one fixed Spin\(^c\)
structure. It has nonzero ordinary SW invariant; \(b_+(M)=1+2gh>1\), so
there is no chamber issue. Thus its unperturbed equations have solutions
for all the metrics in (9), and for every metric conformal to each of them.
The classical Kähler nonvanishing input is recorded in
[LeBrun, Theorem 2](https://arxiv.org/pdf/dg-ga/9412006).

The Kähler form \(\omega_\ell\) of \(g_\ell\) satisfies
\([\omega_\ell]=2\pi c_1(K_M)\). Hodge star on two-forms is unchanged under
the conformal change (9); a closed self-dual form is harmonic for either
metric. Consequently

\[
 \boxed{p_\ell=(a_{\widehat g_\ell}^+)^2=a^2=A=T\quad\text{for every }\ell.}
 \tag{13}
\]

Set

\[
 B_\ell=\int\widehat s_\ell^2\,d\widehat\mu_\ell-32\pi^2A,
 \qquad z_\ell=\Delta f_\ell+|df_\ell|^2.
\]

The conformal factors cancel in the scalar-square integral. Since the
manifold is closed, \(\int\Delta f_\ell=0\), and (10) gives the exact formula

\[
 \boxed{B_\ell=48\int|df_\ell|^2\,d\mu_{g_\ell}
                   +36\int z_\ell^2\,d\mu_{g_\ell}>0.}
 \tag{14}
\]

The strict inequality holds because \(c_-\ne c_+\). By (12),
\(B_\ell=O(\ell)\to0\). This is the ordinary SW scalar defect from
Round 17, not a new weighted defect.

The Weyl-square integral is conformally invariant in dimension four, and
the starting Kähler–Einstein product has
\(\int|W^+|^2=2V_0/3\). Hence the LB defect satisfies the exact identity

\[
 \boxed{D_\ell=\frac{B_\ell}{96\pi^2}\longrightarrow0.}
 \tag{15}
\]

Subtract the Chern–Weil formula for \(g_\ell\) from that for
\(\widehat g_\ell\), keeping this Weyl integral fixed. It gives

\[
 \boxed{P_\ell=\int|\operatorname{Ric}^{\circ}_{\widehat g_\ell}|^2
       =\frac{B_\ell}{12}=8\pi^2D_\ell\longrightarrow0,}
 \tag{16}
\]
\[
 \boxed{R_{2,\ell}=8\pi^2 A+\frac{B_\ell}{3}
                    =8\pi^2 A+32\pi^2D_\ell.}
 \tag{17}
\]

These metrics also approach the absolute Ricci-energy infimum on this
minimal general-type smooth manifold, by
[LeBrun, Theorem 4.3](https://arxiv.org/pdf/math/0003068).

They are genuinely non-Kähler, not merely non-Kähler for the displayed
complex structure. If \(\widehat g_\ell\) were Kähler in the given
orientation, \(|W^+|^2=\widehat s_\ell^2/24\) would force \(B_\ell=0\),
contradicting (14). The same argument applies in the other orientation:
the starting product is Kähler for either orientation and has the same
\(W^-\)-energy. Thus \(\widehat g_\ell\) is not Kähler for either orientation,
although it is conformally Kähler for both.

## 5. The scalar variance and relative defect have positive limits

Define constants

\[
 H_0=V_-+V_+,\qquad H_2=V_-c_-^2+V_+c_+^2,\qquad
 H_4=V_-c_-^4+V_+c_+^4.
 \tag{18}
\]

Outside \(N_\ell\), the scalar is exactly \(-4c_\pm^{-2}\) and the volume
form is \(c_\pm^4d\mu_{g_\ell}\). The transition region has volume
\(O(\ell)\), uniformly bounded scalar, and bounded conformal factor.
Using (7) gives

\[
 \widehat V_\ell\to H_4,\qquad
 \int\widehat s_\ell\,d\widehat\mu_\ell\to-4H_2,\qquad
 \int\widehat s_\ell^2\,d\widehat\mu_\ell\to16H_0.
 \tag{19}
\]

Therefore, with \(L_\ell=\int(\widehat s_\ell-\overline{\widehat s}_\ell)^2\),

\[
 \boxed{L_\ell\longrightarrow
 16\left(H_0-\frac{H_2^2}{H_4}\right)
 =\frac{16V_-V_+(c_-^2-c_+^2)^2}{H_4}>0.}
 \tag{20}
\]

Minimization over nonzero constant Einstein factors gives, as in
[Round 21](round21_comparison.md),

\[
 \mathcal E(\widehat g_\ell)
 =\frac{4P_\ell+L_\ell}{R_{2,\ell}}
 \longrightarrow
 \boxed{4\left[1-
 \frac{(V_-c_-^2+V_+c_+^2)^2}
 {(V_-c_-^4+V_+c_+^4)(V_-+V_+)}\right]}>0.
 \tag{21}
\]

For instance, take \(g_-=g_+=1\), \(h=2\), \(c_-=1\), and
\(c_+=\sqrt2\). The two side volumes are equal and the limiting relative
defect is exactly \(2/5\). A sufficiently wide but fixed transition gives
the negative scalar band (11).

The example can also be globally rescaled to mean scalar \(-4\). Indeed
\(\kappa_\ell=-\overline{\widehat s}_\ell/4\to H_2/H_4>0\), and the metric
\(\kappa_\ell\widehat g_\ell\) has that mean scalar. All energies in
(13)–(21) are unchanged by this constant rescaling; the scalar band remains
uniform. With the mean normalization, the averaged Einstein residual obeys

\[
 \frac{\int|\operatorname{Ric}+\kappa_\ell\widehat g_\ell|^2}
      {\kappa_\ell^2\widehat V_\ell}
 \longrightarrow4\left(\frac{H_0H_4}{H_2^2}-1\right)>0.
 \tag{22}
\]

Thus the failure is not removed by fixing the mean scalar or the volume
scale. The missing information concerns communication between the two
bulk regions.

## 6. The first eigenvalue and the actual SW amplitude

Use \(f_\ell\), after subtracting its \(\widehat g_\ell\)-mean, as a scalar
Rayleigh test. Its Dirichlet energy is

\[
 \int|df_\ell|_{\widehat g_\ell}^2d\widehat\mu_\ell
 =\int e^{2f_\ell}|df_\ell|_{g_\ell}^2d\mu_{g_\ell}=O(\ell),
\]

whereas its variance has a positive limit because its two bulk constants
are unequal. Hence

\[
 \lambda_1(\widehat g_\ell)=O(\ell)\longrightarrow0.
 \tag{23}
\]

There is no conflict with actual SW control. Since \(p=a^2\), the right
side of the ordinary identity (1) is precisely \(B_\ell\to0\). Any ordinary
solution satisfies
\(\|\widehat s_\ell+|\Phi_\ell|^2\|_2\to0\) and
\(\|\nabla_{A_\ell}\Phi_\ell\|_2\to0\), while its amplitude follows the
two different bulk scalar values. Small gradient energy does not force a
single global amplitude across this degenerating bottleneck.

Likewise, the harmonic representative of the canonical class is the same
differential form \(\pm\omega_\ell/(2\pi)\) before and after (9). Its
pointwise norm after conformal scaling is
\(\sqrt2 e^{-2f_\ell}/(2\pi)\), with two different bulk limits, even though
its squared \(L^2\) norm remains exactly \(p=A\). Primitive-class control
fixes a cohomological norm; it does not fix the pointwise amplitude.

## 7. Optional stronger variant: pointwise trace-free Ricci tends to zero

The fixed-window proof already suffices. One may also let
\(R_\ell=\tfrac12\log(1/\ell)\to\infty\), and choose

\[
 f_\ell(t)=\log c_-+
 \log(c_+/c_-)\,\chi(t/R_\ell),
\]

where \(\chi\) is a fixed smooth step equal to zero for inputs at most
\(-1\) and one for inputs at least \(1\). For small \(\ell\), this fits
inside (6). Its transition volume is \(O(\ell e^{R_\ell})=O(\sqrt\ell)\),
and

\[
 \|df_\ell\|_\infty=O(R_\ell^{-1}),\qquad
 \|\nabla^2f_\ell\|_\infty=O(R_\ell^{-1}).
\]

For the Hessian, the collar eigenvalues are \(f''\), \(\tanh(t)f'\), and
two zeros. The conformal trace-free Ricci formula is

\[
 \operatorname{Ric}^{\circ}_{\widehat g_\ell}
 =-2\bigl(\nabla^2f_\ell-df_\ell\otimes df_\ell\bigr)_0.
 \tag{24}
\]

The trace-free operation may be taken with \(g_\ell\), since it is
unchanged under conformal scaling of the metric. The conformal factors
are uniformly bounded above and below, so
\(\|\operatorname{Ric}^{\circ}_{\widehat g_\ell}\|_\infty=O(R_\ell^{-1})\to0\).
The scalar band holds for all sufficiently small \(\ell\). Formula (14)
gives \(B_\ell=O(\sqrt\ell/R_\ell^2)\to0\), and all limits (19)–(22)
remain unchanged. Thus even pointwise convergence of the trace-free Ricci
tensor to zero does not produce one global Einstein factor here.

## 8. Precise consequence and non-consequence

This disproves a uniform estimate tending to zero for scalar variance or
relative Einstein defect based only on LB defect, ordinary SW scalar
defect, period excess, topological cost, a fixed negative scalar band,
bounded volume, and even uniform local curvature bounds. All those inputs
have the required uniform bounds or vanish along this fixed-topology
sequence. A scalar spectral gap or another genuinely global connectivity
hypothesis cannot simply be deleted from the scalar-band stability theorem.

It does not disprove the valid Ricci/connection bridge (1)–(4), the
classical exact equality theorem, or stability under stronger compactness
conditions. Nor is it a Yau26 counterexample: each \(M\) here admits the
original product Einstein metric. Finally, the construction is an explicit
application of classical conformal and hyperbolic geometry; no assertion
is made that this mechanism or its mathematical conclusion is new in the
literature.



---

## 原稿 7: round11_closed_gluing.md

来源：`mathematics/current/round11_closed_gluing.md`；范围：`full`。

原文件 SHA-256：`70c4d9686c5f040051d9c5942fd2b26bb90361e35a8f35a78f943cc44d25af75`。

# Fixed closed near-Einstein metrics from sheared product blocks

21 September 2026.

## 1. The closed construction

Fix finitely many compact connected oriented surfaces \(B_v\), each with
nonempty boundary and \(\chi(B_v)<0\), and closed oriented surfaces \(F_v\)
of a common genus \(p\ge2\). Pair all boundary components in a connected
graph. The block associated to a vertex is \(B_v\times F_v\).
For each oriented edge, fix a gluing diffeomorphism

\[
\Psi_e:S^1\times F_v\longrightarrow S^1\times F_w,\qquad
(\phi,x)\longmapsto(-\phi+u_e(x),f_e(x)),
\tag{1}
\]

where the circle coordinate has period \(2\pi\),
\(u_e:F_v\to\mathbb R/(2\pi\mathbb Z)\) is smooth and
\(f_e:F_v\to F_w\) is any orientation-preserving diffeomorphism.
The minus sign makes the boundary identification orientation reversing.
Let \(M\) be the resulting fixed closed oriented smooth four-manifold.
There is no condition that the mapping classes of the \(f_e\) have finite
order, commute, or extend over adjacent product blocks.

**Theorem.** The fixed manifold \(M\) carries smooth metrics \(G_\ell\),
\(\ell\downarrow0\), with

\[
\begin{aligned}
\|\operatorname{Ric}_{G_\ell}+3G_\ell\|_{\infty,\mathrm{op}}
&\le C\bigl(|\log\ell|^{-1}+\ell\bigr)\longrightarrow0,\\
\operatorname{Vol}(M,G_\ell)&=\frac{4\pi^2}{9}\chi(M),\\
\|\nabla^j\operatorname{Rm}_{G_\ell}\|_\infty&\le C_j
 \qquad(j\ge0).
\end{aligned}
\tag{2}
\]

The norm in the first line is the endomorphism norm formed using
\(G_\ell\). The same bound, with constants depending on \(j\), holds
for every fixed covariant derivative order of the defect. All gluing
maps, integral shear classes and smooth identifications are fixed.
Constants may depend on these finite data.

Rescaling \(\widehat G_\ell=3G_\ell\) gives the normalization
\(\|\widehat G_\ell^{-1}\operatorname{Ric}_{\widehat G_\ell}+I\|_\infty
\to0\), with \(\operatorname{Vol}(\widehat G_\ell)=4\pi^2\chi(M)\).

The construction uses two separated operations: a slow hyperbolic-metric
path absorbs the possibly infinite-order \(f_e\), and a shrinking circle
suppresses the integral shear. Their supports are disjoint, so no
uncontrolled interaction between them is hidden in (2).

## 2. Exact hyperbolic boundary collars

Fix curvature-\(-3\) metrics \(h_v\) on \(F_v\). Give every \(B_v\) a
curvature-\(-3\) metric \(b_{v,\ell}\) with geodesic boundary circles
of length \(\ell\). Choose these by a fixed pants decomposition with
fixed internal length/twist parameters and all boundary lengths \(\ell\).
The half-collars inside the blocks are disjoint and have the form

\[
dt^2+a_\ell(t)^2d\phi^2,\qquad
a_\ell(t)=\frac{\ell}{2\pi}\cosh(\sqrt3\,t),
\qquad
0\le t<w_\ell,
\tag{3}
\]

where one may use

\[
w_\ell=\frac1{\sqrt3}
\operatorname{arsinh}\!\left(\frac1{\sinh(\sqrt3\,\ell/2)}\right).
\tag{4}
\]

This is the usual collar lemma after scaling. For a precise version,
see [Rupflin--Topping, Appendix A, Lemma A.1](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1605.06691).
The boundary version follows by doubling the hyperbolic surface across
its geodesic boundary.

Put \(L_\ell=w_\ell/2\). In a signed edge collar
\([-L_\ell,L_\ell]\times S^1\times F_v\), use coordinates on the left
and pull right coordinates back by (1). The pullback of the unmodified
right product is

\[
dt^2+a_\ell(t)^2(d\phi+\beta_e)^2+f_e^*h_w,
\qquad
\beta_e=-u_e^*(d\phi).
\tag{5}
\]

The form \(\beta_e\) is a global closed one-form on \(F_v\), with
periods in \(2\pi\mathbb Z\). It need not be exact. The circle-valued
map in (1), not a real-valued primitive of \(\beta_e\), supplies the
actual global endpoint identification.

## 3. Absorbing arbitrary mapping classes

Choose a fixed smooth path \(h_{e,z}\), \(0\le z\le1\), of
curvature-\(-3\) metrics on the marked \(F_v\), with

\[
h_{e,0}=h_v,\qquad h_{e,1}=f_e^*h_w.
\tag{6}
\]

Such paths exist by connectedness of the space of marked hyperbolic
metrics. They can, for example, be constructed by smooth uniformization
of a path of positive definite metrics. Each fixed path and all its
finite-order derivatives have uniform bounds on the compact parameter
interval. No global isotopy from the identity to \(f_e\) is asserted.

Choose a fixed smooth step function \(\zeta\), constant zero and one
near the endpoints of \([0,1]\). On \([-L_\ell,-1]\), set

\[
z(t)=\zeta\!\left(\frac{t+L_\ell}{L_\ell-1}\right),\qquad
g=dt^2+a_\ell(t)^2d\phi^2+h_{e,z(t)}.
\tag{7}
\]

Use \(h_v\) before the transition and \(f_e^*h_w\) afterwards.
There is no connection shear in this region.
For \(S=\tfrac12 h^{-1}\dot h\), \(H=\operatorname{tr}S\), and
\(k=a_\ell'/a_\ell\), the full Ricci defect has blocks

\[
\begin{aligned}
E_{tt}&=-\dot H-\operatorname{tr}(S^2),\\
E_{UU}&=-kH,\qquad U=a_\ell^{-1}\partial_\phi,\\
E_{FF}^{\sharp}&=-D_tS-(k+H)S,\\
E_{tF}&=\operatorname{div}_h S-dH,\\
E_{tU}&=E_{UF}=0,
\end{aligned}
\tag{8}
\]

where \(E=\operatorname{Ric}+3g\) and \(D_tS\) is the covariant
time derivative. These are the complete Gauss--Codazzi blocks,
including the trace terms. The path need not have a common pointwise
area form; therefore no endpoint-changing Moser isotopy is required.

We have \(S,H,\operatorname{div}S,dH=O(L_\ell^{-1})\),
\(\dot H,D_tS=O(L_\ell^{-2})\), and
\(|k|\le\sqrt3\). Hence (8) gives
\(\|E\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le C/L_\ell\).
All metric-path constants refer to a fixed compact family, not to
a family approaching the boundary of fibre Teichmüller space.
The collapsing circle introduces no inverse-\(a_\ell\) loss: coefficients
are independent of \(\phi\), and its connection uses \(k\), not
\(a_\ell^{-1}\) alone.

## 4. Absorbing integral shear at the collapsed throat

Choose a fixed smooth \(z_s(t)\), zero for \(t\le0\), one for \(t\ge1\),
constant on neighborhoods of the endpoints. On this region keep
\(h=f_e^*h_w\) fixed and set

\[
g=dt^2+a_\ell(t)^2\bigl(d\phi+z_s(t)\beta_e\bigr)^2+h.
\tag{9}
\]

Now the circle quotient metric is \(dt^2+h\), its connection is
\(\alpha=z_s\beta_e\), and its curvature is
\(F=z_s'\,dt\wedge\beta_e\).
Let \(b=\beta_e\), \(D=\operatorname{div}_h b\) (divergence of the
dual vector field), \(a=a_\ell\), and \(k=a'/a\).
In the orthonormal splitting \((\partial_t,U,TF)\), the nonzero defect
blocks are

\[
\begin{aligned}
E_{tt}&=-\tfrac12a^2(z_s')^2|b|^2,&
E_{UU}&=\tfrac12a^2(z_s')^2|b|^2,\\
E_{XY}&=-\tfrac12a^2(z_s')^2b(X)b(Y),&
E_{tU}&=\tfrac12a z_s'D,\\
E_{XU}&=-\tfrac12a(z_s''+3kz_s')b(X),&
E_{tX}&=0.
\end{aligned}
\tag{10}
\]

The mixed entries follow from
\(\operatorname{Ric}(X,K)=(2a)^{-1}\nabla^j(a^3F_{Xj})\),
with \(K=\partial_\phi\), then division by \(a\).
The factor \(3k\) cannot be omitted. The horizontal, vertical and
mixed blocks are all in the actual metric.

The off-diagonal part is a single row/column coupling to \(U\).
Consequently

\[
\|E\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le
\frac{a^2(z_s')^2|b|^2}{2}
+\frac a2
\sqrt{(z_s')^2D^2+(z_s''+3kz_s')^2|b|^2}.
\tag{11}
\]

On the fixed interval \(0\le t\le1\), \(a\le C\ell\).
The form \(b\), metric \(h\), and profile are fixed, so this is
\(O(\ell)\). If \(b\) happens to be harmonic, the \(tU\) entry
vanishes, but harmonicity is not needed.
Outside this interval \(F=0\); the local gauge change of circle
coordinate makes (9) an exact hyperbolic product. At the right
endpoint the global change is precisely the circle-valued gluing (1).

## 5. Smooth closed assembly, volume and curvature bounds

Use the product \(b_{v,\ell}+h_v\) outside the disjoint edge collars,
and formulas (7), (9) within each. The fibre path is complete before
the shear starts. At every interface the interpolation profiles are
constant on a neighborhood, so all derivatives match.
On the right side the metric agrees with the actual pullback (5);
there is no replacement of a nontrivial mapping class or integral
shear by an isotopic identity. This constructs a positive smooth
metric on the fixed marked closed \(M\).

The finite number of disjoint active collars gives the maximum of
their error bounds, proving the first line of (2).
The circle shear has determinant one. Although the fibre path need
not preserve its area form pointwise, every \(h_{e,z}\) has the same
total area by Gauss--Bonnet. Integrating each collar over the closed
fibre therefore gives exactly the original product volume.
Thus

\[
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{Vol}(G_\ell)
&=\sum_v\operatorname{Area}(B_v,b_{v,\ell})
             \operatorname{Area}(F_v,h_v)\\
&=\frac{4\pi^2}{9}\sum_v\chi(B_v)\chi(F_v)
 =\frac{4\pi^2}{9}\chi(M).
\end{aligned}
\tag{12}
\]

The boundaries have zero Euler characteristic, so the last equality
is the ordinary gluing formula. In particular, \(\chi(M)>0\).

In a unit frame on the circle and a fixed finite atlas on each fibre,
the connection coefficients on the path region use only bounded
fibre-family coefficients, \(k\), and \(S\).
On the shear region they additionally use \(a z_s'\beta_e\), its
fixed-order derivatives, and the fixed base connection.
No coefficient depends on the circle coordinate.
Repeated differentiation therefore gives uniform bounds for all
fixed-order curvature derivatives, and retains an \(L_\ell^{-1}\)
or \(\ell\) factor in each defect derivative.
Outside active regions the metric is locally a symmetric product.

There is a stronger local conclusion. Put
\(\varepsilon_\ell=L_\ell^{-1}+\ell\). On each active collar let
\(\mathcal R_0\) be the algebraic curvature tensor of the orthogonal
product of two curvature-\(-3\) planes, using the actual splitting
into the \((t,U)\)-plane and the horizontal fibre plane. If \(P\) is
the projection onto the first plane, then

\[
\begin{aligned}
|\nabla^j(\operatorname{Rm}-\mathcal R_0)|
 &\le C_j\varepsilon_\ell &&(j\ge0),\\
|\nabla^jP|&\le C_j\varepsilon_\ell &&(j\ge1).
\end{aligned}
\tag{13}
\]

In the path region the only mixing of the planes is the shape
operator \(S=O(L_\ell^{-1})\). The difference tensor consists of
the Gauss terms \(S\wedge S\), the normal terms \(D_tS+S^2\),
the circle-fibre terms \(kS\), and the Codazzi derivatives of \(S\).
Their fixed-order derivatives have the same bound.
In the shear region the only extra connection terms are
\(a z_s'\beta_e\); the curvature difference consists of their
first derivatives, their products, and products with the bounded
\(k\) and fibre connection. These are \(O(\ell)\), with the same
fixed-order bounds. The splitting is parallel on the unmodified
product regions. Since \(\mathcal R_0\) is algebraic in the metric
and \(P\), (15) implies the global estimates

\[
-3-C\varepsilon_\ell\le
 \operatorname{sec}_{G_\ell}\le C\varepsilon_\ell,
\qquad
\|\nabla^j\operatorname{Rm}_{G_\ell}\|_\infty
\le C_j\varepsilon_\ell\longrightarrow0
\quad(j\ge1).
\tag{14}
\]

No diameter or injectivity-radius bound follows. In particular,
approximate parallel curvature here does not assert a globally
locally symmetric metric on \(M\).

## 6. Symplectic structures in both orientations

The same closed smooth manifold admits symplectic structures in both
orientations. In fact the construction can be made almost Kähler in
both orientations without changing any of its estimates.

First choose the block area forms \(\omega_v=d\mu_{h_v}\). They have
the same total area. By Moser's argument each \(f_e\) is isotopic
to an \(f'_e\) with \((f'_e)^*\omega_w=\omega_v\).
Replacing an edge gluing by this isotopic gluing does not change the
closed oriented diffeomorphism type: a boundary isotopy extends through
the product collar. The shear function \(u_e\) is retained.
Any metrics or forms constructed with these adjusted gluings can
therefore be pulled back to the original fixed smooth \(M\).

For clarity, there is also an endpoint-fixed version of the
area adjustment of the hyperbolic path. Start with any path \(h_z\)
between the two now area-compatible endpoint metrics, and write
\(\omega_z=d\mu_{h_z}\), \(\omega_0=\omega_1=\omega_v\).
Choose a fixed linear right inverse for \(d\) on the exact two-forms
on the closed fibre, and put

\[
d\sigma_z=\omega_z-\omega_v,\qquad \sigma_0=\sigma_1=0.
\]

For each \(z\), apply Moser's argument in an auxiliary parameter
\(s\in[0,1]\) to
\(\omega_{s,z}=(1-s)\omega_v+s\omega_z\), using
\(\iota_{X_{s,z}}\omega_{s,z}=-\sigma_z\).
All these are positive area forms. Its time-one map \(\psi_z\)
satisfies
\(\psi_z^*\omega_z=\omega_v\); because the vector fields vanish
for \(z=0,1\), both endpoint maps are exactly the identity.
Thus \(\widetilde h_z=\psi_z^*h_z\) is a smooth curvature-\(-3\)
path with the same endpoint metrics and fixed area form \(\omega_v\).
All path bounds remain finite. This auxiliary-parameter construction
avoids assuming that an arbitrary Moser transport around a loop
has identity endpoint.

In the signed collar coordinates the following two-forms now match
the product forms on both incident blocks:

\[
\Omega_\pm
=a_\ell(t)\,dt\wedge\bigl(d\phi+z_s(t)\beta_e\bigr)
 \ \pm\ \omega_v .
\tag{15}
\]

On the preceding metric-path region take \(z_s=0\).
Outside collars use
\(d\mu_{b_{v,\ell}}\pm\omega_v\).
They agree under the actual area-preserving gluing, including its
circle-valued shear. They are closed: \(d\beta_e=0\), the
\(z_s'\) term contains two \(dt\)'s, and the fibre area form is
independent of \(t\). Moreover

\[
\Omega_\pm^2
=\pm2a_\ell\,dt\wedge(d\phi+z_s\beta_e)\wedge\omega_v
\ne0.
\tag{16}
\]

The two signs give the two orientations. With the common-area path,
these forms are compatible with the constructed metric: the horizontal
two-plane and fibre two-plane have their respective metric area forms.
Thus the near-Einstein sequence can also be chosen to carry compatible
almost Kähler structures of both orientations. This is not a claim
that the structures are integrable or that the metric is Einstein.

## 7. Relation to the inherited surface-bundle construction

If every \(f_e\) is the identity, projection to the common \(F\)
survives all gluings. The other factors assemble into the surface
fibre, with boundary rotations encoding multitwist monodromy.
Single-edge examples overlap the earlier \(E_D\) construction after
interchanging the two surface roles. These must not be advertised
as new smooth objects. Fixed isometries generate only finite holonomy
in the isometry group of a closed hyperbolic surface; that case also
requires care before asserting new topology.

The theorem itself allows arbitrary \(f_e\), including pseudo-Anosov
holonomy around graph cycles. The separate topology analysis
examines the self-gluing of \(B_{h,2}\times\Sigma_p\) with nonzero
integral shear and infinite-order \(f\). Its claim of absence of a
surface-bundle structure is not needed for the metric proof above.
This separates the proven construction from the group-theoretic
classification and from the still different question of whether
the resulting smooth manifold admits any Einstein metric.

The metric proof does not assert an Einstein correction or an
all-sign Einstein obstruction. Its advance is a fixed closed input
class with full unweighted Ricci defect tending to zero, rather than
another open-ended connector. The analytic ingredients are familiar
collar and submersion calculations; no literature-priority claim is
made for this assembly.

## 8. Coordinate checks

The accompanying round11_closed_gluing_check.py reconstructs the
Christoffel symbols and Ricci tensor from the coordinate metric. Its
shear test uses
\(h=(dx^2+e^{2x}dy^2)/3\) and \(\beta=dx+dy\), so that
\(\operatorname{div}_h\beta^\sharp=3\ne0\); it checks every entry
of (10), not just the trace. A second hyperbolic-path test uses
\(h_z=(dx^2+e^{2x+2z}dy^2)/3\), with nonzero \(H\), and checks
every entry of (8). Three deliberately incorrect mixed/trace
formulas are rejected. These are local tensor checks; the closed
assembly, fixed marking, estimates and symplectic argument are the
analytic and geometric proofs above.

## 原稿 8: round11_graph_topology.md

来源：`mathematics/current/round11_graph_topology.md`；范围：`full`。

原文件 SHA-256：`6005abf4dd6c17ae71bd91fec042871420bbdb81357a29d7cd2b3310ba8c2324`。

# Sheared product-surface gluings beyond surface bundles

21 September 2026.

## 1. The closed objects

Let \(F_{h,b}\) be a compact connected oriented surface with \(b>0\)
boundary circles and negative Euler characteristic, and let \(\Sigma_g\)
be a closed oriented surface of genus \(g\ge2\). The blocks are
\(F_{h,b}\times\Sigma_g\). Boundary identifications have the form
\[
 (\phi,x)\longmapsto(-\phi+u(x),f(x)),
 \qquad u:\Sigma_g\longrightarrow\mathbb R/2\pi\mathbb Z,
 \quad f\in\operatorname{Diff}^+(\Sigma_g).
 \tag{1.1}
\]
The sign is chosen for an oriented gluing. The homotopy class of \(u\)
is an integral cohomology class; write the corresponding homomorphism
\(\pi_1(\Sigma_g)\to\mathbb Z\) by the same letter.

The metric construction in `round11_closed_gluing.md` is a separate
input. The results below identify its actual smooth manifolds and groups;
they do not assume an Einstein metric or a curvature sign.

Two elementary properties hold for every closed oriented assembly:
\[
 \chi(M)=(2-2g)\sum_v\chi(F_v)>0,
 \qquad \tau(M)=0.
 \tag{1.2}
\]
Euler characteristic is additive because each gluing hypersurface has
Euler characteristic zero. Every product block has signature zero: an
orientation-reversing diffeomorphism of its first surface reverses its
orientation. Novikov additivity then applies across the whole closed
three-dimensional gluing hypersurfaces. There are no corners requiring
a Wall nonadditivity term.

Each block and each boundary component is aspherical, and the boundary
fundamental groups inject into the block groups. The universal cover is
a tree of contractible vertex and edge spaces. Thus \(M\) is aspherical,
and its group is the graph of groups prescribed by (1.1).

## 2. A separating gluing is an earlier surface-bundle object

Suppose there are two blocks \(F_{h_1,1}\times\Sigma_g\) and
\(F_{h_2,1}\times\Sigma_g\), joined along their only boundaries by
(1.1). Use \(f^{-1}\) to identify the second copy of the base with the
first. The projection to \(\Sigma_g\) then extends over the gluing.
The result is a smooth bundle
\[
 \Sigma_{h_1+h_2}\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow\Sigma_g.
 \tag{2.1}
\]
For a loop \(\gamma\) in the base its monodromy is
\(D_c^{\pm u(\gamma)}\), where \(c\) is the separating seam.
To see this, locally lift \(u\) to a real angle and absorb that angle
in a collar of the seam. Two lifts differ by an integral full rotation;
the transition on the closed fibre is the corresponding power of the
Dehn twist. This is a rank-one multitwist bundle of the earlier
\(E_D\) type, with the two surface roles interchanged.

This does **not** mean that a nonzero shear extends over a product block.
In fact it does not, as the following group calculation shows.

**Nonextension lemma.** Let \(F\) have negative Euler characteristic,
\(c\) be a boundary loop, and \(u\ne0\). The boundary map (1.1) does
not extend to a diffeomorphism of \(F\times\Sigma_g\).

Write \(\pi_1(F\times\Sigma_g)=A\times\Gamma\), with \(A\) a
nonabelian free group and \(\Gamma=\pi_1(\Sigma_g)\). The centralizer
of \(\{1\}\times\Gamma\) is \(A\times\{1\}\). The boundary shear
maps this subgroup, up to conjugacy, to
\[
 \Gamma_u=\{(c^{u(\gamma)},f_*(\gamma)):\gamma\in\Gamma\}.
\]
Since \(f_*\) is onto and \(u\ne0\), the centralizer of \(\Gamma_u\)
is \(C_A(c^k)\times\{1\}\cong\mathbb Z\), for any nonzero value
\(k\) of \(u\). The centralizer of a nontrivial element of a free group
is cyclic. An automorphism induced by an extension would have to identify
these two centralizers, which is impossible. This also excludes an
extension not preserving the product factors.

## 3. A cyclic gluing which is not any surface bundle over a surface

Take a **single** block
\[
 B=F_{h,2}\times\Sigma_g,\qquad h\ge1,\quad g\ge2,
\]
and identify its two boundary components by (1.1). Denote the resulting
closed manifold by \(M(h,g;u,f)\). Put
\[
 A=\pi_1(F_{h,2})\cong F_{2h+1},\qquad \Gamma=\pi_1(\Sigma_g),
\]
and write \(c_1,c_2\in A\) for the two boundary elements. With suitable
base paths, its fundamental group is the HNN extension
\[
 G=\left\langle A\times\Gamma,t\;\middle|\;
 t c_1t^{-1}=c_2^{-1},\quad
 t\gamma t^{-1}=c_2^{u(\gamma)}f_*(\gamma)
 \ (\gamma\in\Gamma)\right\rangle .
 \tag{3.1}
\]
Changing base paths changes the representatives by conjugacy, not the
statements below. The edge injections are injective, so the vertex group
\(A\times\Gamma\) embeds in \(G\).

**Theorem.** If \(u\ne0\) and \([f_*]\) has infinite order in
\(\operatorname{Out}(\Gamma)\), there is no exact sequence
\[
 1\longrightarrow\pi_1(\Sigma_p)\longrightarrow G
   \longrightarrow\pi_1(\Sigma_q)\longrightarrow1,
 \qquad p,q\ge2.
 \tag{3.2}
\]
Consequently \(M(h,g;u,f)\) is not the total space of an oriented closed
surface bundle over an oriented closed surface.

**Proof.** Suppose \(\psi:G\to\Pi_q\) has kernel \(K\cong\Pi_p\),
where \(\Pi_j\) is a closed hyperbolic surface group. Such groups contain
no \(\mathbb Z^2\); the centralizer of a nonidentity element is cyclic;
and the centralizer of a nonabelian subgroup is trivial.

The subgroups \(\psi(A)\) and \(\psi(\Gamma)\) commute. If neither is
nonabelian, both are cyclic or trivial. Both restrictions then have
nontrivial kernels: a nonabelian free group and a closed hyperbolic
surface group cannot inject into a cyclic group. Choose nonidentity
elements in these two kernels. Their copies in the direct product
generate \(\mathbb Z^2\subset K\), a contradiction. Hence one of the
two images is nonabelian and the other is trivial.

If \(\psi(A)\) is nonabelian, then \(\Gamma\subset K\). Normality of
\(K\), together with the second HNN relation, implies
\(c_2^{u(\gamma)}\in K\) for every \(\gamma\in\Gamma\). Choose
\(\gamma\) for which \(u(\gamma)\ne0\). This nontrivial element of
\(A\) commutes with \(\Gamma\subset K\), again yielding
\(\mathbb Z^2\subset K\).

If \(\psi(\Gamma)\) is nonabelian, then \(A\subset K\). The
restriction \(r=\psi|_\Gamma\) is injective: a nonidentity element of
its kernel would commute with a nonidentity element of \(A\), producing
the same contradiction. An embedded closed hyperbolic surface group in
another closed hyperbolic surface group has finite index. Indeed an
infinite-index subgroup is the group of a noncompact covering surface
and is free, whereas \(\Gamma\) is not free.

Let \(a=\psi(t)\). Since \(\psi(c_2)=1\), (3.1) gives
\[
 a r(\gamma)a^{-1}=r(f_*(\gamma)).
\]
The subgroup \(r(\Gamma)\) is normalized by \(a\) and has finite index.
Some positive power \(a^N\) therefore lies in \(r(\Gamma)\). Iterating
the displayed equation and using injectivity of \(r\) shows that
\(f_*^N\) is inner. This contradicts the hypothesis on \([f_*]\),
and proves (3.2).

For the bundle consequence, (1.2) gives \(\chi(M)>0\). Euler
characteristic multiplicativity eliminates a torus fibre or base and
the opposite-sign sphere/hyperbolic combinations. The only remaining
nonhyperbolic possibility is a sphere bundle over a sphere, inconsistent
with the embedded infinite vertex group. Thus any such bundle would
give (3.2). \(\square\)

This excludes **every** oriented surface-bundle structure on this
manifold, not merely the two projections suggested by its construction.
It does not assert that no finite cover is a surface bundle.

## 4. Fully specified small examples

The theorem requires infinite mapping-class order, not a pseudo-Anosov
mapping class.

For one explicit choice, take \(h=1\), \(g=2\), let \(\alpha\) be a
nonseparating curve in \(\Sigma_2\), take \(f=D_\alpha\), and choose
\(u\) to be the primitive algebraic-intersection functional with
\(\alpha\). This specifies all gluing data in (3.1).

There is also an **even-first-Betti-number** example. Split \(\Sigma_2\)
into two once-holed tori, and on each use a boundary-fixing mapping class
whose homology matrix is
\[
 H=\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Such a map is a product of two Dehn twists with chosen signs. The product
\(f\) of the two disjointly supported maps has homology action
\(F=\operatorname{diag}(H,H)\). It has infinite order, since \(H\) has
an eigenvalue greater than one. Choose any primitive nonzero \(u\).

Here is the abelianization calculation, including the shear. Writing
\(c=[c_2]\), one has \([c_1]=-c\), so the first HNN relation adds no
new abelian relation. The second relation imposes
\[
 (I-f_*)x-u(x)c=0,\qquad x\in H_1(\Sigma_g;\mathbb Z).
 \tag{4.1}
\]
Thus
\[
 H_1(M;\mathbb Z)=\mathbb Z^{2h+1}\oplus
 \frac{\mathbb Z c\oplus H_1(\Sigma_g;\mathbb Z)}
 {\langle (I-f_*)x-u(x)c\rangle}.
 \tag{4.2}
\]
The \(2h+1\) free summands outside the fraction are the \(2h\) handle
generators and the HNN stable letter.

In the first example, \(f_*-I\) has image generated by
\([\alpha]\), with coefficient \(u\). Equation (4.1) eliminates one
primitive combination of \(c\) and \([\alpha]\). In the second example
\(\det(I-F)=1\), so (4.1) eliminates all four surface-homology
generators in favour of \(c\), with no torsion. Consequently:

| Example | \(\chi\) | \(\tau\) | \(b_1\) | \(b_2\) | \(b_2^+=b_2^-\) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Single twist, dual shear | 4 | 0 | 7 | 16 | 8 |
| Two hyperbolic homology blocks, primitive shear | 4 | 0 | 4 | 10 | 5 |

The exact checker `round11_graph_topology_check.py` computes the integral
Smith normal forms of these two presentation matrices. Their nonzero
invariant factors are all one, so neither abelianization has torsion.
It also verifies the displayed Betti numbers and the symplectic condition
on the two homology actions. This check does not replace the group proof
in Section 3.

The second example makes clear that odd Betti-number parity is not the
basis of the nonbundle theorem. Related smooth types may already arise
from cut-and-paste or Luttinger-surgery constructions; no classification
or novelty of these smooth manifolds is asserted.

## 5. Which global obstructions do and do not apply

An Einstein metric on any of these manifolds must have negative Einstein
constant. Positive Ricci curvature would make the fundamental group
finite; Ricci-flatness on a closed manifold would make it virtually
abelian. Both are incompatible with its embedded nonabelian free group.
This is a sign reduction, not negative-Einstein nonexistence.

The familiar higher-graph results cannot be applied by name alone.
Connell--Suarez-Serrato's [Definition 1](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1208.4876)
requires infranil fibres over pinched-negative bases. Our closed
hyperbolic surface fibre is not infranil, and its boundary group
\(\mathbb Z\times\Pi_g\) is nonamenable. In particular their
infranil-fibre collapse and amenable-wall simplicial-volume arguments
do not establish corresponding statements here.

Contreras Peruyero--Suarez-Serrato's
[Theorems 5--6](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2107.14380) concern, respectively,
pieces of geometries \(\mathbb H^3\times\mathbb R\),
\(\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}}_2\times\mathbb R\),
\(\mathbb H^2\times\mathbb R^2\), and higher-graph classes with the
specified collapse hypotheses. Their proof uses \(\chi=\tau=0\).
Our pieces have geometry \(\mathbb H^2\times\mathbb H^2\), and
\(\chi(M)>0\), so that proof does not supply a contradiction.

The separate `round11_kahler.md` establishes a Kähler-group obstruction
using the Bass--Serre action and a primary tree-factorization theorem.
Its finite-cover argument uses the normal finite-index core to preserve
minimality of the action. This is stronger information about complex
geometry, but it still cannot be substituted for an obstruction to
arbitrary negative Einstein metrics.

The exact global problem is now sharper: the closed near-Einstein
construction applies to explicit aspherical manifolds **outside all
oriented surface bundles over surfaces**, not only to a reparametrized
\(E_D\) family. Deciding arbitrary Einstein existence on these same
smooth manifolds remains open in this work. The topology results above
do not silently restrict the potential Einstein metric to preserve the
graph decomposition, either surface factor, or any circle action.



---

## 原稿 9: round11_kahler.md

来源：`mathematics/current/round11_kahler.md`；范围：`full`。

原文件 SHA-256：`929f73660e5bdfd1638cc785761b58402714c76d94b9477e6f002d62128274d6`。

# A virtual Kähler obstruction for the closed sheared gluings

21 September 2026.

## 1. Statement and geometric consequence

Let \(M=M(h,g;u,f)\) be obtained from
\(F_{h,2}\times\Sigma_g\), \(h\ge1\), \(g\ge2\), by identifying
its two boundary components by
\[
 (\phi,x)\longmapsto(-\phi+u(x),f(x)).
\]
Here \(f\) is orientation preserving and \(u:\Sigma_g\to S^1\)
has nonzero integral cohomology class. No order condition on \([f]\)
is needed in this note.

**Theorem.** No finite-index subgroup of \(\pi_1(M)\) is a Kähler
group. In particular, no finite cover of \(M\), with either orientation,
admits any Kähler structure.

Combined with [the closed construction](round11_closed_gluing.md), this
gives fixed closed aspherical four-manifolds with uniformly bounded
curvature and all fixed curvature derivatives, with
\[
 \|G_\ell^{-1}\operatorname{Ric}_{G_\ell}+3I\|_\infty\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad \operatorname{Vol}(G_\ell)=\frac{4\pi^2}{9}\chi(M),
\]
but with no Kähler structure even after passage to a finite cover. If
\([f]\) has infinite order, the separate
[group argument](round11_graph_topology.md#3-a-cyclic-gluing-which-is-not-any-surface-bundle-over-a-surface)
also excludes every oriented closed surface-bundle structure on \(M\).
These are different conclusions, proved by different arguments.

The Kähler obstruction does not exclude an arbitrary negative Einstein
metric. It removes a concrete class of global existence mechanisms;
it does not impose complex structure on a hypothetical Einstein metric.

## 2. The tree and its kernel

Set \(A=\pi_1(F_{h,2})\), \(\Gamma=\pi_1(\Sigma_g)\), and
\(V=A\times\Gamma\). Write \(c_1,c_2\in A\) for the boundary
elements. The group is
\[
 G=\left\langle V,t\ \middle|\
 tc_1t^{-1}=c_2^{-1},\quad
 t\gamma t^{-1}=c_2^{u(\gamma)}f_*(\gamma),\ \gamma\in\Gamma
 \right\rangle .                                      \tag{1}
\]
Both edge injections are injective. Let \(T\) be the Bass--Serre tree
and \(v\) the vertex stabilized by \(V\). One adjacent edge has
stabilizer \(E_1=\langle c_1\rangle\times\Gamma\). The other
embedding also has image \(E_2=\langle c_2\rangle\times\Gamma\):
the powers of \(c_2\) already belong to the edge group and remove the
shear in its second factor.

Both \(E_i\) have infinite index in \(V\). Hence \(T\) is not a
line. The action is minimal. One direct verification is that the stable
letter has an axis through \(v\); every nonempty invariant subtree
contains this axis and therefore, by vertex transitivity, every vertex.
There is no fixed end: if \(V\) fixed an end, it would fix the first
edge of the unique ray from \(v\) to that end. This would put \(V\)
inside a proper adjacent edge stabilizer.

Let \(C=\ker(G\curvearrowright T)\). Since it fixes every edge,
\[
 C\subseteq\bigcap_{a\in A}aE_1a^{-1}=\Gamma.             \tag{2}
\]
Indeed the normal core of a peripheral cyclic subgroup in the
nonabelian free group \(A\) is trivial. This follows, for example,
by choosing an element outside its cyclic normalizer and intersecting
the two conjugates. Boundary elements in this surface group are not
proper powers.

The kernel \(C\) is normal in \(G\). For \(\gamma\in C\), (1)
and (2) give
\[
 c_2^{u(\gamma)}f_*(\gamma)=t\gamma t^{-1}\in C\subseteq\Gamma.
\]
The two direct factors of \(V\) intersect trivially, and \(c_2\)
has infinite order. Consequently
\[
                 C\subseteq\Gamma\cap\ker u.             \tag{3}
\]
We do not need to identify \(C\) exactly. It may be infinitely
generated; replacing it by a surface group would be incorrect.

## 3. The Kähler-group input

The tree factorization theorem of Gromov--Schoen, in the precise form
stated in [Nicolás, Theorem 25 and Lemma 24, p. 10 of the PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2102.01128),
has the following consequence. If a compact Kähler group acts minimally
on a tree other than a point or a line, without fixing an end, there is
an exact sequence
\[
 1\longrightarrow N\longrightarrow H
   \longrightarrow\Pi^{\rm orb}\longrightarrow1,          \tag{4}
\]
where \(\Pi^{\rm orb}\) is a closed hyperbolic orbifold group,
\(N\) is finitely generated, and \(N\) acts trivially on the tree.
Finite generation follows because \(N\) is the image of the
fundamental group of the compact generic fibre of the associated
holomorphic map. The result applies without assuming a faithful
tree action. The original source is
[Gromov--Schoen, Publ. Math. IHÉS 76 (1992), 165--246](https://numdam.org/item/PMIHES_1992__76__165_0/).

We also use the following elementary surface-group fact: a nontrivial
finitely generated normal subgroup of a closed hyperbolic surface group
has finite index. In the covering-surface description, a finitely
generated infinite-index subgroup is free and has a finite compact
core. A nontrivial normal subgroup cannot have such a proper compact
core: equivalently its limit set, invariant under the whole cocompact
surface group, is the entire boundary circle, whereas an infinite-index
finitely generated subgroup has proper limit set. A cyclic normal
subgroup is already excluded by cyclic centralizers.

## 4. Proof for every finite cover

Let \(H<G\) have finite index and suppose it is a Kähler group.
We first check the tree hypotheses for \(H\), rather than assume
they pass to finite index without proof.

Let \(H_0\) be the normal finite-index core of \(H\) in \(G\).
A positive power of \(t\) belongs to \(H_0\), so \(H_0\) contains
a hyperbolic tree isometry. Its minimal subtree exists and is unique,
being the convex hull of its hyperbolic axes. Normality makes this
subtree \(G\)-invariant. Minimality of \(G\) therefore makes it
all of \(T\). Thus \(H\) acts minimally as well.

If \(H\) fixed an end, \(H\cap V\) would stabilize the first edge
of the ray from \(v\). This is impossible: \(H\cap V\) has finite
index in \(V\), whereas every adjacent edge stabilizer has infinite
index in \(V\). The tree is still not a point or a line.

Apply (4). The group \(N\) lies in
\[
 \ker(H\curvearrowright T)=H\cap C
       \subseteq (H\cap\Gamma)\cap\ker u.                 \tag{5}
\]
Set \(\Gamma_H=H\cap\Gamma\), a finite-index closed surface
subgroup of \(\Gamma\). The group \(N\) is normal in
\(\Gamma_H\), because it is normal in \(H\). It is finitely
generated, so it is either trivial or finite index in \(\Gamma_H\).
The second case is impossible. A nonzero homomorphism
\(u:\Gamma\to\mathbb Z\) remains nonzero on every finite-index
subgroup: for any element on which it is nonzero, a positive power
belongs to that subgroup. Equation (5) says instead that it vanishes
on \(N\).

Therefore \(N=1\). Equation (4) would identify \(H\) with a
hyperbolic orbifold group. But \(V=A\times\Gamma\) contains
\(\mathbb Z^2\), generated by a nonidentity element in each factor.
Its intersection with \(H\) is finite index in \(\mathbb Z^2\).
A cocompact Fuchsian orbifold group contains no \(\mathbb Z^2\).
This contradiction proves the theorem. \(\square\)

## 5. What a hypothetical Einstein metric would have to be

The construction and obstruction apply, in particular, to the explicit
example in `round11_graph_topology.md` with
\[
 \chi(M)=4,\quad\tau(M)=0,\quad b_1(M)=4,\quad b_2^+=b_2^-=5.
\]
Thus the complex-geometric obstruction is not merely odd parity of
\(b_1\).

Any Einstein metric on this \(M\) would have negative Einstein
constant. The positive case is excluded by Myers' theorem and the
infinite fundamental group. The Ricci-flat case is excluded by the
virtually abelian fundamental group theorem for closed nonnegative-Ricci
manifolds: \(G\) contains a nonabelian free subgroup. Neither inference
places restrictions on the unknown negative Einstein metric.

The theorem also excludes every Einstein metric becoming Kähler on a
finite cover. In particular the usual locally symmetric alternatives
are unavailable: a closed real-hyperbolic metric would contradict the
embedded \(\mathbb Z^2\); a complex-hyperbolic metric has a Kähler
finite cover; and a metric locally isometric to
\(\mathbb H^2\times\mathbb H^2\) has a finite cover preserving both
factor orientations, hence a parallel product complex structure.

The unresolved alternative is a genuinely non-Kähler negative Einstein
metric, not necessarily close to the constructed family, not necessarily
respecting its cutting hypersurfaces, and not necessarily having
nonpositive sectional curvature. Excluding such metrics requires a
further argument. If even one of the explicit examples admits no such
metric, its constructed sequence supplies a negative-sign Yau26
counterexample. This alone would not negate the project's unrestricted
interval formulation, which also requires failure of the positive sign
in the same dimension.

The useful new connection is that a shrinking-circle shear costs
arbitrarily little Ricci error but has a permanent effect on the
fundamental group: it traps the kernel of the tree action in an
infinite-index surface subgroup. That is the link between the metric
construction and the virtual Kähler obstruction.

## 原稿 10: round11_variational.md

来源：`mathematics/current/round11_variational.md`；范围：`full`。

原文件 SHA-256：`6bada093b7ce5c33d8daf2f811b3fa73f6a77fa092139b595019e2e89c47b9d6`。

# Exact variational values on the closed sheared gluings

21 September 2026.

## 1. Objects, normalization and conclusions

Let \(M=M(h,g;u,f)\) be the self-gluing of
\(F_{h,2}\times\Sigma_g\), where \(h\ge1\), \(g\ge2\),
\(u\ne0\), and \(f\) is orientation preserving, as defined in
`round11_graph_topology.md`. No order condition on \([f]\) is needed
here. The established inputs are:

* \(\chi=4h(g-1)>0\), \(\tau=0\), and \(b_1\ge1\);
* symplectic forms in both orientations, constructed in Section 6 of
  `round11_closed_gluing.md`;
* no Kähler structure on any finite cover, proved in
  `round11_kahler.md`;
* smooth metrics \(g_j=3G_{\ell_j}\) on this fixed closed manifold with

\[
 V_j=V_0:=4\pi^2\chi,
 \qquad
 \delta_j:=\|g_j^{-1}\operatorname{Ric}_{g_j}+I\|_{\infty,\mathrm{op}}
 \longrightarrow0.
 \tag{1}
\]

In either orientation,
\(b_2^\pm=\chi/2-1+b_1\ge2\). Thus no chamber-dependent
\(b^+=1\) assertion is needed. Write
\(E_g=\operatorname{Ric}_g-s_g g/4\). All curvature tensors below
use the full covariant tensor norm; in particular
\(|\operatorname{Rm}|^2=\sum_{i,j,k,l}R_{ijkl}^2\), not the norm of
the curvature operator on \(\Lambda^2\).

**Theorem.** These manifolds satisfy

\[
\begin{aligned}
\sigma(M)&=-8\pi\sqrt\chi,\\
\inf_{\operatorname{Ric}_g\ge-g}\operatorname{Vol}(M,g)
 &=4\pi^2\chi,\\
\inf_g\int_M s_g^2\,d\mu_g&=64\pi^2\chi,\\
\inf_g\int_M|\operatorname{Ric}_g|^2\,d\mu_g&=16\pi^2\chi,\\
\inf_g\int_M|\operatorname{Rm}_g|^2\,d\mu_g&=32\pi^2\chi.
\end{aligned}
\tag{2}
\]

No conformal class realizes the Yamabe invariant \(\sigma(M)\).
The constrained-volume, scalar-energy and Ricci-energy infima are
also not attained. In contrast, attainment of the full-curvature
infimum is **equivalent to existence of some Einstein metric on
\(M\)** and is not decided here.

These values result from applying classical monopole estimates to the
closed construction. They do not assert a new general monopole
inequality or an all-Einstein obstruction.

## 2. The scalar negative-part estimate and its equality case

Choose either symplectic orientation and let \(a\) be the first
Chern class of its associated almost-complex structure, with either
choice of sign. Then

\[
 a^2=2\chi+3\tau=2\chi>0.
 \tag{3}
\]

[Taubes, *The Seiberg--Witten invariants and symplectic forms*,
Math. Res. Lett. 1 (1994), 809--822, Main Theorem](https://doi.org/10.4310/MRL.1994.v1.n6.a15)
gives a nonzero Seiberg--Witten invariant for this class when
\(b^+>1\). For every metric the unperturbed equations therefore
have a solution. One may obtain it as a limit of small generic
perturbations; reducibility is excluded since
\((a_g^+)^2\ge a^2>0\).

Set \(s_-:=\max\{-s,0\}\). The conventions for the monopole
equations, the quadratic map and the Weitzenböck formula are those
in [LeBrun, Section 2, equations (2)--(6)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/math/0003068):
\(|F_A^+|^2=|\Phi|^4/8\) and

\[
 0=\int_M\bigl(4|\nabla_A\Phi|^2+s|\Phi|^2+|\Phi|^4\bigr)\,d\mu.
\]

Consequently

\[
 \int|\Phi|^4
 \le\int s_-|\Phi|^2
 \le\left(\int s_-^2\right)^{1/2}
       \left(\int|\Phi|^4\right)^{1/2}.
\]

The spinor is nonzero. Hodge projection of the determinant curvature
then gives

\[
 \boxed{
 \int_M s_-^2\,d\mu
 \ge\int_M|\Phi|^4\,d\mu
 =8\int_M|F_A^+|^2\,d\mu
 \ge32\pi^2(a_g^+)^2
 \ge64\pi^2\chi.}
 \tag{4}
\]

The last inequality uses
\((a_g^+)^2=a^2+\|a_g^-\|_{L^2}^2\), with the harmonic-form
normalization for the real cohomology class. This argument uses the
negative part, so it does not assume that an arbitrary metric has
nonpositive scalar curvature.

Equality in (4) forces equality in the first integrated inequality.
Thus \(\nabla_A\Phi=0\). Its quadratic two-form is a nonzero
parallel self-dual form of constant length, which defines a parallel
orthogonal complex structure. The metric is Kähler. In particular,
equality is impossible on the present \(M\). The same conclusion
holds if \(\int s^2=64\pi^2\chi\), since
\(\int s^2\ge\int s_-^2\).

## 3. Yamabe invariant and nonattainment

For a conformal class \([g]\), use

\[
 Y([g])=\inf_{v>0}
 \frac{\int_M(6|dv|_g^2+s_gv^2)\,d\mu_g}
      {(\int_Mv^4\,d\mu_g)^{1/2}},
 \qquad \sigma(M)=\sup_{[g]}Y([g]).
\]

The Yamabe theorem supplies a smooth constant-scalar-curvature
minimizer in each class; see
[Schoen, J. Differential Geometry 20 (1984), 479--495](https://math.jhu.edu/~js/Math646/schoen.yamabe.pdf).
Equation (4) excludes nonnegative scalar curvature, so this minimizer
has negative scalar curvature. For it,
\(Y([g])^2=\int s^2\ge64\pi^2\chi\). Therefore

\[
 Y([g])\le-8\pi\sqrt\chi.
 \tag{5}
\]

For the explicit family (1),
\(s_{g_j}\ge-4(1+\delta_j)\). Hölder's inequality, with this
negative lower bound, gives for every positive \(v\)

\[
 \frac{\int(6|dv|^2+s_{g_j}v^2)}{(\int v^4)^{1/2}}
 \ge-4(1+\delta_j)V_0^{1/2}.
\]

Hence \(Y([g_j])\to-8\pi\sqrt\chi\), proving the first value
in (2). If a class attained this supremum, its Yamabe metric would
saturate (4), hence be Kähler. The Kähler obstruction excludes this.
Nonattainment concerns the supremum over conformal classes, not the
existence of the Yamabe minimizer inside any individual class.

## 4. Ricci-lower-bound minimal volume

Let
\(\mathcal V_{\mathrm{Ric}}(M)=
\inf\{\operatorname{Vol}(M,g):\operatorname{Ric}_g\ge-g\}\).
For any admissible metric, \(s_g\ge-4\), so \(s_-^2\le16\).
Combining this with (4) proves

\[
 16\operatorname{Vol}(M,g)
 \ge\int_Ms_-^2\,d\mu_g
 \ge64\pi^2\chi.
 \tag{6}
\]

To attain the infimum as a limit, set
\(\widetilde g_j=(1+\delta_j)g_j\). Constant rescaling leaves
the covariant Ricci tensor unchanged, so

\[
 \operatorname{Ric}_{\widetilde g_j}
 =\operatorname{Ric}_{g_j}
 \ge-(1+\delta_j)g_j=-\widetilde g_j,
 \qquad
 \operatorname{Vol}(\widetilde g_j)
 =(1+\delta_j)^2V_0\longrightarrow V_0.
\]

This proves \(\mathcal V_{\mathrm{Ric}}(M)=V_0\).
If equality were attained in (6), then \(s_-\equiv4\), hence
\(s\equiv-4\). Together with \(\operatorname{Ric}\ge-g\),
this already forces \(\operatorname{Ric}=-g\). Equality in (4)
also forces a Kähler structure, a contradiction.

The identical argument gives
\(\inf_{s_g\ge-4}\operatorname{Vol}(M,g)=V_0\), also unattained.
Replacing \(s_-^2\) by \(s^2\) in the lower-bound argument would
be invalid: a Ricci lower bound does not exclude positive scalar spikes.

## 5. Three curvature energies, two different equality mechanisms

Equation (4) gives the scalar-energy lower bound. For Ricci energy,

\[
 |\operatorname{Ric}|^2=|E|^2+\frac{s^2}{4}
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \int|\operatorname{Ric}|^2\ge16\pi^2\chi.
 \tag{7}
\]

Along (1), \(s_{g_j}\to-4\) and
\(|\operatorname{Ric}_{g_j}|^2\to4\) uniformly, while the
volumes are exactly \(V_0\). This attains both lower bounds as
limits. Equality for scalar energy would give a Kähler metric.
Equality for Ricci energy would additionally force \(E=0\).
Neither infimum is attained on \(M\).

For the full curvature tensor, the four-dimensional
Chern--Gauss--Bonnet identity is instead

\[
 32\pi^2\chi
 =\int_M\bigl(|\operatorname{Rm}|^2
               -4|\operatorname{Ric}|^2+s^2\bigr)\,d\mu,
\]

or, equivalently,

\[
 \boxed{\int_M|\operatorname{Rm}|^2\,d\mu
        =32\pi^2\chi+4\int_M|E|^2\,d\mu.}
 \tag{8}
\]

The convention agrees with the curvature-operator Chern--Weil
formulas in [LeBrun, equation (1)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/math/0003068)
after multiplying the curvature-operator squared norm by four.
The trace-free projection is orthogonal, so (1) implies
\(|E_{g_j}|^2\le4\delta_j^2\). Fixed volume makes its integral
tend to zero, proving the last infimum in (2).

Equation (8) has equality precisely when \(E=0\). On a connected
four-manifold, the contracted Bianchi identity then makes the scalar
curvature constant. Thus equality is precisely the Einstein condition.
It imposes **no scalar-energy equality** in (4). Accordingly, the
nonattainment conclusions above do not prove nonattainment in (8).

## 6. The remaining Einstein alternative

The fundamental group excludes positive and Ricci-flat Einstein
metrics, as shown in `round11_kahler.md`. Any remaining Einstein
metric can be normalized to \(\operatorname{Ric}=-g\). For such
a metric the results above imply strictly

\[
 \operatorname{Vol}(M,g)>4\pi^2\chi,
 \quad Y([g])=-4\sqrt{\operatorname{Vol}(M,g)}<\sigma(M),
 \quad \int|\operatorname{Rm}|^2=32\pi^2\chi.
 \tag{9}
\]

A negative constant-scalar-curvature metric is the Yamabe minimizer
in its own conformal class. There is no conflict between the strict
Yamabe inequality in (9) and its being Einstein: it need not maximize
the Yamabe invariant over all conformal classes. Likewise it need
not minimize volume among all metrics with \(\operatorname{Ric}\ge-g\).

The smallest even-\(b_1\) example has \(\chi=4\), so
\(\sigma=-16\pi\), \(\mathcal V_{\mathrm{Ric}}=16\pi^2\),
and the three energy infima are respectively
\(256\pi^2,64\pi^2,128\pi^2\). The first two are unattained;
attainment of the third is the remaining global Einstein question.

All these formulas also hold on any connected finite cover, with
its own Euler characteristic. Pullback preserves the symplectic forms
and the uniform defect, multiplies volume and Euler characteristic
by the covering degree, and the virtual Kähler obstruction supplies
the same nonattainment conclusion. The stable-letter homomorphism
has nonzero restriction to every finite-index subgroup, so the cover
still has \(b_1\ge1\) and hence \(b^+>1\).
