## 原稿 11: round12_global.md

来源：`mathematics/current/round12_global.md`；范围：`full`。

原文件 SHA-256：`c600524cc97a8bfd6685c62fa026c5e4d6309c7137737bc1d62b708808895204`。

# Canonical classes and the full ordinary Seiberg--Witten basic set

21 September 2026.

## 1. Statement

Let \(M=M(h,g;u,f)\), \(h\ge1\), \(g\ge2\), be obtained by
self-gluing the two boundary components of
\(B=F_{h,2}\times\Sigma_g\) by
\((\phi,x)\mapsto(-\phi+u(x),f(x))\). The gluing is as in
`round11_graph_topology.md`; in particular \(u\ne0\) in integral
cohomology and \(f\) is orientation preserving. We use the symplectic
forms and the closed metric sequence
constructed in `round11_closed_gluing.md`.

Here \(\chi=4h(g-1)\ge4\), while the stable-letter quotient
\(\pi_1(M)\to\mathbb Z\) gives \(b_1\ge1\). Since \(\tau=0\),
\(b^+=b^-=\chi/2+b_1-1\ge2\); the chamber-independent gauge
theory and Taubes theorem below therefore apply in both orientations.

The two symplectic forms determine an oriented splitting
\(TM=E\oplus F\) into real two-plane bundles. Here \(E\) is tangent
to the first surface factor on block interiors and to the
\((t,\phi)\) planes on the neck; \(F\) is the complementary plane
bundle used by the symplectic pair. Set

\[
 x=e(E),\qquad y=e(F),\qquad a=x+y,\qquad b=x-y.
 \tag{1}
\]

Thus \(a=c_1(TM,J_+)\) and \(b=c_1(TM,J_-)\); the corresponding
canonical bundle classes are \(-a\) and \(-b\).
All unqualified intersection products use the \(J_+\) orientation.

**Theorem.** The canonical data satisfy

\[
 x^2=y^2=0,\qquad x\cdot y=\chi(M),
 \qquad
 \begin{pmatrix}a^2&a\cdot b\\a\cdot b&b^2\end{pmatrix}
 =\begin{pmatrix}2\chi&0\\0&-2\chi\end{pmatrix}.
 \tag{2}
\]

The ordinary Seiberg--Witten basic first-Chern classes are exactly

\[
 \boxed{\mathcal B(M)=\{a,-a\},\qquad
        \mathcal B(-M)=\{b,-b\}.}
 \tag{3}
\]

These are statements about integral first-Chern classes, not only
their real images. No order condition on \([f]\), and no rank condition
on \(I-f_*\), is required. The argument does not enumerate distinct
Spin\(^c\) structures sharing a first-Chern class when two-torsion
is present. In the explicit torsion-free example from Round 11 that
ambiguity does not arise.

The proof uses the actual near-Einstein construction to constrain
the entire basic set; it is not a computation by formal gluing of
Seiberg--Witten polynomials. It also gives, in both orientations,

\[
 \alpha^2(M)=\beta^2(M)=2\chi(M)
 \tag{4}
\]

for LeBrun's period-plane minimax and monopole convex-hull invariants.
Equation (4) concerns all monopole classes, potentially more than
the ordinary basic classes in (3).

This calculation does not exclude an arbitrary negative Einstein
metric. It determines the available ordinary basic classes and shows
that the numerical convex-hull and period-plane refinements cannot
raise the topological monopole budget above \(2\chi\).

## 2. Euler classes of the two plane bundles

The gluing preserves \(E\): derivatives of the circle-valued shear
mix the transverse plane into the circle direction, but do not mix
an \(E\)-vector into the transverse plane. In signed normal and
circle coordinates, its restriction to \(E\) is the constant matrix
\(-I\). This observation permits a particularly simple connection.

Choose a connection on \(TF_{h,2}\) which is flat in the product
frames on its boundary collars. Pull it back to \(E\) in the block.
Its flat collar connections glue across the constant transition
\(-I\). The resulting global Euler form is supported in the block
interior and is pulled back from a two-form on \(F_{h,2}\).
Its square vanishes pointwise. Thus \(x^2=0\).

The Euler and Pontryagin identities for an oriented two-plane
splitting give

\[
 e(TM)=x y,\qquad p_1(TM)=x^2+y^2.
\]

Since \(\tau(M)=0\), their evaluations give \(xy=\chi\) and
\(y^2=0\). Expanding (1) proves (2). In particular the two canonical
classes are not two positive-square basic classes in one orientation:
the second one is a basic class for the opposite orientation.

## 3. Energy saturation constrains every basic class

Write \(Q=2\chi>0\). Round 11 supplies metrics \(g_j\) with

\[
 \frac{1}{32\pi^2}\int_M s_{g_j}^2\,d\mu_{g_j}
       =:A_j\longrightarrow Q.
 \tag{5}
\]

For each monopole class \(c\), the scalar-curvature estimate gives
\((c_{g_j}^+)^2\le A_j\). The estimate and its normalization are
proved from the monopole equations in `round11_variational.md`,
Section 2; the primary source is
[LeBrun, *Ricci Curvature, Minimal Volumes, and Seiberg--Witten Theory*, Section 2](https://arxiv.org/pdf/math/0003068).

If \(c\) is an ordinary basic class, its nonzero invariant requires
nonnegative expected dimension

\[
 d(c)=\frac{c^2-(2\chi+3\tau)}4=\frac{c^2-Q}4\ge0.
\]

Negative-dimensional generic moduli spaces are empty. Conversely,
the projection estimate implies \(c^2\le A_j\), hence \(c^2\le Q\).
It follows that every ordinary basic class has

\[
 c^2=Q,\qquad d(c)=0,
 \qquad \|c_{g_j}^-\|^2=(c_{g_j}^+)^2-Q\longrightarrow0.
 \tag{6}
\]

Here \(\|c^-\|^2=-c^-\cdot c^-\). The same holds for the
canonical class \(a\). Cauchy--Schwarz on the two Hodge summands
therefore gives

\[
 |a\cdot c|
 \le\sqrt{(a_{g_j}^+)^2(c_{g_j}^+)^2}
       +\|a_{g_j}^-\|\,\|c_{g_j}^-\|
 \le A_j+(A_j-Q).
\]

Taking the limit proves the metric-independent constraint

\[
                  |a\cdot c|\le Q.                       \tag{7}
\]

This argument obtains simple type directly from the sharp
scalar-energy sequence, so a separate simple-type theorem is not
being assumed.

## 4. Product tori and the neck subspace

Every product of embedded loops in the interior of \(F_{h,2}\)
and in \(\Sigma_g\) is an embedded torus with trivial normal
bundle. The adjunction inequality forces every basic class to have
zero pairing with such a torus. If a particular torus is torsion
or nullhomologous, the same zero pairing is automatic. The precise
self-intersection-zero torus consequence is stated in
[Fintushel--Stern, *Knots, Links, and 4-Manifolds*, Section 2, p. 9](https://arxiv.org/pdf/dg-ga/9612014).

The product tori span
\(H_1(F_{h,2};\mathbb R)\otimes H_1(\Sigma_g;\mathbb R)\).
Künneth's theorem and \(H^2(F_{h,2};\mathbb R)=0\) therefore give

\[
 c|_B=d\,\eta,\qquad a|_B=(2-2g)\eta,                    \tag{8}
\]

where \(\eta\) is the pullback of the integral generator of
\(H^2(\Sigma_g)\), and \(d=c[\{p\}\times\Sigma_g]\).
Since \(2-2g\ne0\), every basic class can be written over
\(\mathbb R\) as

\[
 c=t a+r,\qquad
 t=\frac d{2-2g},\qquad
 r\in R:=\ker\bigl(H^2(M;\mathbb R)\to H^2(B;\mathbb R)\bigr).
 \tag{9}
\]

Cut along the nonseparating gluing hypersurface
\(Y=S^1\times\Sigma_g\). The Mayer--Vietoris connecting map
\(\delta:H^1(Y;\mathbb R)\to H^2(M;\mathbb R)\) has image
\(R\). Its classes have representatives
\(\rho(t)dt\wedge\theta\), where \(\theta\) is closed on
\(Y\), and \(\rho\) is supported in a small neck with
\(\int\rho\,dt=1\). Any two such representatives have zero
wedge product. Consequently

\[
                         R\cdot R=0.                     \tag{10}
\]

Use (6) in (9). If \(t=0\), (10) would give \(c^2=0\),
contrary to \(Q>0\). For \(t\ne0\),

\[
 Q=t^2Q+2t(a\cdot r),\qquad
 a\cdot c=\frac Q2\left(t+\frac1t\right).                 \tag{11}
\]

For a real nonzero \(t\), \(|t+t^{-1}|\ge2\), with equality
only for \(t=\pm1\). Combining (7) and (11) forces

\[
              c=\pm a+r,\qquad r\in R,\qquad a\cdot r=0. \tag{12}
\]

Thus neither a different fibre evaluation nor a nonzero canonical
pairing in the neck subspace can produce another basic class.

## 5. Taubes extremality removes the remaining neck classes

Fix one of the positive-orientation symplectic forms \(\Omega\)
from Round 11. On any neck slice \(Y\), its restriction is the
fibre area form \(\omega\); the term containing \(dt\) restricts
to zero. Thus

\[
 [\Omega|_Y]=\operatorname{Area}(\Sigma_g,\omega)\eta,
 \qquad a|_Y=(2-2g)\eta.
\]

The connecting-map pairing, evaluated using the representatives
above, gives for every \(r=\delta[\theta]\in R\)

\[
 r\cdot[\Omega]
 =\frac{\operatorname{Area}(\Sigma_g,\omega)}{2-2g}\,
      r\cdot a.                                         \tag{13}
\]

The common sign from the orientation convention for \(\delta\)
cancels in the ratio. Equation (12) therefore implies
\(|c\cdot[\Omega]|=|a\cdot[\Omega]|\).

[Taubes, *More Constraints on Symplectic Forms from Seiberg--Witten Invariants*, Theorem 2](https://archive.intlpress.com/site/pub/files/_fulltext/journals/mrl/1995/0002/0001/MRL-1995-0002-0001-a002.pdf)
states that the canonical bundle class uniquely maximizes the absolute
symplectic pairing among basic classes, up to sign. Its equality
conclusion is an equality of integral classes. Since that canonical
class is \(-a\), (12)--(13) force \(c=\pm a\) integrally.
Both signs occur, and their invariants have absolute value one by
Taubes's theorem. This proves the first assertion of (3).

The same argument applies in the opposite symplectic orientation.
There \(b^2_{-M}=Q\), the scalar-energy limit is unchanged, the
neck subspace is still isotropic, and the restriction of the
symplectic form to \(Y\) is again proportional to the restriction
of \(b\). It yields \(\mathcal B(-M)=\{b,-b\}\).

## 6. A direct check in the smallest even-Betti example

For completeness, the example \(h=1,g=2\) with
\(f_* =\operatorname{diag}(H,H)\),
\(H=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&1\\1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\),
has a shorter real-cohomology conclusion before invoking Taubes
extremality. Its neck exact sequence is

\[
 H^1(B;\mathbb R)\xrightarrow{D}H^1(Y;\mathbb R)
 \xrightarrow{\delta}H^2(M;\mathbb R)\longrightarrow H^2(B;\mathbb R).
\]

The image of \(D\), in
\(H^1(Y)=\mathbb R[d\phi]\oplus H^1(\Sigma_g)\), is

\[
              \operatorname{im}(I-f^*)+\mathbb R[u].      \tag{14}
\]

There is no circle component: the two boundary evaluations of a
one-form on \(F_{h,2}\) are opposite, and the gluing reverses the
circle. Its remaining contribution is a multiple of \(u\).
Here \(\det(I-f_*)=1\), so \(R\) is one-dimensional, generated
by \(\delta[d\phi]\), with \(d\phi\) normalized to have
period one. Its pairing with \(a\) is
\(\pm(2-2g)\ne0\). Hence \(R\cap a^\perp=0\), and (12)
already gives \(c=\pm a\) over \(\mathbb R\).

Round 11's integer Smith-normal-form computation gives
\(H_1(M;\mathbb Z)=\mathbb Z^4\), so \(H^2(M;\mathbb Z)\)
has no torsion. This also gives the integral conclusion directly.
The example has
\(\chi=4\), \(\tau=0\), \(b_1=4\), \(b^+=b^-=5\), and
the canonical Gram matrix is \(\operatorname{diag}(8,-8)\).

## 7. Convex-hull and minimax values, and the Einstein question

Let \(\mathcal C(M)\subset H^2(M;\mathbb R)\) be the finite
set of nonzero monopole classes. The definitions of \(\beta^2\)
and \(\alpha^2\) are those in
[LeBrun, *Four-Manifolds, Curvature Bounds, and Convex Geometry*, Definition 4.3 and Section 5](https://arxiv.org/pdf/math/0611450):

\[
 \beta^2=\max_{v\in\operatorname{Hull}(\mathcal C)}v^2,
 \qquad
 \alpha^2=\inf_{H}\max_{c\in\mathcal C}(c_H^+)^2,
\]

where \(H\) ranges over maximal positive subspaces of the real
intersection form. For a convex combination \(v\) of monopole
classes, the projection norm is at most \(\sqrt{A_j}\) by (5)
and the triangle inequality. Thus \(v^2\le A_j\), implying
\(\beta^2\le Q\). The actual Hodge planes of \(g_j\) are
admissible in the minimax, so \(\alpha^2\le\lim A_j=Q\).
The canonical monopole class has square \(Q\), which gives both
opposite inequalities. This proves (4). Reverse orientation gives
the same result.

Equation (3) does not classify monopole classes invisible to ordinary
Seiberg--Witten invariants, nor the Bauer--Furuta invariant. Equation
(4) nevertheless bounds every monopole class in the numerical schemes
specified there. A further gauge-theoretic obstruction would need
information beyond simply increasing these two numbers.

The two symplectic orientations also cannot be pooled into a single
positive-square basic set: (2) records their opposite intersection
signs. In particular, the calculation leaves intact the remaining
possibility from Round 11 of a genuinely non-Kähler negative Einstein
metric with normalized volume strictly above \(4\pi^2\chi\).
No adapted splitting, holomorphic structure, or graph-preserving
property has been imposed on such an unknown metric.



---

## 原稿 12: round17_product_operator.md

来源：`mathematics/current/round17_product_operator.md`；范围：`full`。

原文件 SHA-256：`9466afc1842fb88fd23559c41ddd5d74e93071f1ced7efd5046a40ddef5b9fff`。

# Full tensor reduction on degenerating hyperbolic products

## 1. Scope, normalization, and relation to existing theory

Let \((B,g_B)\) and \((F,g_F)\) be arbitrary connected closed oriented
surfaces of genera \(b,f\ge2\), both of constant curvature \(-3\).
Put \(M=B\times F\), \(g_0=g_B+g_F\). Thus
\(\operatorname{Ric}_{g_0}=-3g_0\), but mixed sectional curvatures are
zero. Either factor may degenerate, and their genera need not be bounded.

Use nonnegative scalar and Hodge Laplacians. With
\(\delta h=-\nabla^ih_{ij}\), \(\delta^*\alpha=\frac12\mathcal L_{
\alpha^\sharp}g\), and \(\mathcal B h=\delta h+\frac12d\operatorname{tr}h\),
the full gauged operator is
\[
 P_0=D(\operatorname{Ric}+3g)_{g_0}+\delta^*\mathcal B
     =\tfrac12\Delta_L+3
     =\tfrac12\nabla^*\nabla-\mathring R.                    \tag{1}
\]
It acts on all symmetric two-tensors, not merely TT tensors. Our
curvature convention is \(\mathring Rg=\operatorname{Ric}\).

Product spectra and stability are classical. Kröncke's
[*On infinitesimal Einstein deformations*, Proposition 4.1 and §4](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1508.00721)
give the tensor-product spectrum, the relevant commuting identities,
and the negative-Einstein product kernel. The calculations below
specialize this structure to two-dimensional hyperbolic factors, with
an explicit proof of the surface identity, precise degenerating channels,
and uniform perturbative estimates. No new general product-stability
theorem or literature priority is claimed.

Koiso's [1980 rigidity paper](https://ir.library.osaka-u.ac.jp/repo/ouka/all/26044/04842_Dissertation.pdf)
explicitly excludes two-dimensional factors from its noncompact-symmetric
rigidity assertion. Those factors have the genuine deformation spaces
retained below.

This is also outside [Jäckel's Theorem 1.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2502.16692v2):
that theorem assumes strictly negative sectional curvature bounded away
from zero, and sectional curvature exactly \(-1\) in the thin part.
The products here satisfy neither assumption. Its uniform inverse theorem
cannot be imported into this problem by replacing the word hyperbolic
with product-hyperbolic.

## 2. The complete surface calculation

For either surface \(\Sigma\), define the trace-free conformal-Killing
operator
\[
 D_\Sigma\alpha=\delta^*\alpha+\tfrac12(\delta\alpha)g_\Sigma.
\]
On trace-free tensors its adjoint is \(D_\Sigma^*=\delta\).
Let
\[
 \mathcal T_\Sigma
   =\{q:\operatorname{tr}q=0,\ \delta q=0\}.
\]
These are the real parts of holomorphic quadratic differentials, with
\(\dim\mathcal T_\Sigma=6\operatorname{genus}(\Sigma)-6\).

The exact surface identities are
\[
 \boxed{
 P_\Sigma|_{S^2_0}=D_\Sigma\delta,
 \qquad \delta D_\Sigma=\tfrac12\Delta_H+3,
 \qquad P_\Sigma(ug_\Sigma)
       =(\tfrac12\Delta u+3u)g_\Sigma.}                     \tag{2}
\]
Here \(\Delta_H=\nabla^*\nabla-3\) on one-forms, since
\(\operatorname{Ric}=-3g\). In particular this is the Hodge Laplacian,
not the unshifted rough Laplacian.

One can derive (2) by commuting two derivatives and inserting
\(\mathring Rq=3q\) for trace-free surface tensors. More explicitly,
\[
 D\delta q=\tfrac12\nabla^*\nabla q-3q,
 \qquad
 \delta D\alpha=\tfrac12\nabla^*\nabla\alpha+\tfrac32\alpha.
\]
These expressions also follow directly in the local metric
\((dx^2+dy^2)/(3y^2)\) for arbitrary tensor entries, as checked by the
accompanying coordinate program. They are tensorial identities and apply
on every closed quotient.

The orthogonal decomposition is
\[
 L^2(S^2\Sigma)
  =L^2(\Sigma)g_\Sigma
       \ \widehat\oplus\ \mathcal T_\Sigma
       \ \widehat\oplus\ \overline{D_\Sigma\Omega^1(\Sigma)}.
                                                               \tag{3}
\]
In fact \(D\) has closed range from \(H^1\) to \(L^2\), because
\[
 \|D\alpha\|_2^2
  =\tfrac12\langle\Delta_H\alpha,\alpha\rangle
       +3\|\alpha\|_2^2.
                                                               \tag{4}
\]
The orthogonal complement of its range among trace-free tensors is
exactly \(\ker\delta=\mathcal T_\Sigma\). Equation (2) shows
\(Pq=0\) for \(q\in\mathcal T_\Sigma\), while
\[
 P D\alpha=D(\tfrac12\Delta_H+3)\alpha.                    \tag{5}
\]
Thus every nonzero surface branch outside \(\mathcal T_\Sigma\)
has eigenvalue at least \(3\).

For completeness, an orthonormal basis of one-forms consists of harmonic
forms and \(\lambda_i^{-1/2}du_i\),
\(\lambda_i^{-1/2}*du_i\) for positive scalar eigenvalues
\(\Delta u_i=\lambda_i u_i\). Applying
\(D(\frac12\Delta_H+3)^{-1/2}\) is an isometry onto the last
summand of (3). This normalization avoids treating a differential map
as an unweighted unitary identification.

## 3. Every product tensor sector

Let \(u_i\), \(v_j\) be scalar eigenbases on \(B,F\), with
eigenvalues \(\lambda_i,\nu_j\), including the normalized constant
functions at index zero. Let \(\alpha_r,\gamma_s\) be Hodge one-form
eigenbases with eigenvalues \(\mu_r,\xi_s\), including all harmonic
one-forms. Let \(q_a\), \(r_d\) be bases of
\(\mathcal T_B,\mathcal T_F\).

The following is a complete orthogonal system after the normalizations
in (3)--(5). A displayed tensor may be rescaled to norm one without
changing its eigenvalue.

| Tensor | Eigenvalue of \(P_0\) |
| --- | --- |
| \(q_a\,v_j\) | \(\nu_j/2\) |
| \(u_i\,r_d\) | \(\lambda_i/2\) |
| \(u_i v_j g_B\), \(u_i v_j g_F\) | \(3+(\lambda_i+\nu_j)/2\) |
| \((D_B\alpha_r)v_j\) | \(3+(\mu_r+\nu_j)/2\) |
| \(u_i(D_F\gamma_s)\) | \(3+(\lambda_i+\xi_s)/2\) |
| \(\alpha_r\odot\gamma_s\) | \(3+(\mu_r+\xi_s)/2\) |

Here \(\alpha\odot\gamma=(\alpha\otimes\gamma+
\gamma\otimes\alpha)/\sqrt2\), so tensor products of unit
one-forms have unit norm.

To verify completeness, use the orthogonal bundle splitting
\[
 S^2(T^*M)=S^2(T^*B)\oplus S^2(T^*F)
                         \oplus(T^*B\odot T^*F)
\]
and apply (3) to the first two blocks. Product covariant derivatives
preserve all three blocks. On a pure \(B\) tensor, \(P_0\) is
\(P_B+\frac12\Delta_F\). On a mixed tensor the curvature action
vanishes, while each rough one-form Laplacian is \(\Delta_H+3\).
This gives the last row, including the positive shift for harmonic
one-forms. There is no omitted mixed zero mode. The relative scaling
tensor \(g_B-g_F\) also has eigenvalue \(3\), not zero.

Define the entire exceptional channel
\[
 \mathcal L=
 [\mathcal T_B\widehat\otimes L^2(F)]
       \widehat\oplus
 [L^2(B)\widehat\otimes\mathcal T_F],
 \qquad \mathcal H=\mathcal L^\perp.                         \tag{6}
\]
All of \(\mathcal L\) is TT on the four-dimensional product. Multiplying
a fiber TT tensor by a base function creates no divergence because
the nonzero tensor slots remain entirely in the fiber.

**Complete complement theorem.** Independently of injectivity radius,
diameter, volume, and genus,
\[
 \boxed{\langle P_0h,h\rangle\ge3\|h\|_2^2
            \quad(h\in\mathcal H\cap H^1),}               \tag{7}
\]
whereas on \(\mathcal L\) the operator is exactly
\[
 \boxed{P_0|_{\mathcal L}
      =\tfrac12\Delta_F\text{ on each }\mathcal T_B
       \ \oplus\ \tfrac12\Delta_B\text{ on each }\mathcal T_F.}
                                                               \tag{8}
\]
The true kernel is only
\[
 \ker P_0=\mathcal T_B\otimes\mathbb R
           \oplus\mathbb R\otimes\mathcal T_F,
 \qquad \dim\ker P_0=6b+6f-12.                              \tag{9}
\]
These are the genuine factor Teichmüller deformations, and they integrate
to hyperbolic product metrics. Removing (9) does **not** remove the rest
of (6).

## 4. Exact inverse channels and their unavoidable degeneration

For a forcing orthogonal to (9), the inverse in the first channel is
\[
 \sum_a q_a\,F_a(y)\longmapsto
      2\sum_a q_a\,\Delta_F^{-1}F_a(y),\qquad \int_F F_a=0,
                                                               \tag{10}
\]
and the second channel uses \(2\Delta_B^{-1}\) in precisely the same
way. These are vector-valued scalar equations, not a finite list of
constant parameters.

The remaining inverse is diagonal in the complete table above. After
Hodge decomposition and the normalized \(D\) identification, every
coefficient is obtained from a positive shifted scalar resolvent
\[
 \bigl(3+\tfrac12(\Delta_B+\Delta_F)\bigr)^{-1},            \tag{11}
\]
or its specialization with a harmonic one-form label fixed. This is an
exact \(L^2\) description; reconstruction involves normalized differential
maps and does not automatically preserve maximum norms.

The optimal \(L^2\) spectral gap after removing only the kernel is
\[
 \boxed{\min\{3,\lambda_1(B)/2,\lambda_1(F)/2\}.}         \tag{12}
\]
For a separating hyperbolic degeneration, \(\lambda_1\to0\).
This last fact can be seen without a compactness theorem: take constants
on the two thick pieces and join them harmonically across the collar.
For \(a=c\cosh(kt)\), the least radial transition energy is
\[
 \frac{2\pi}{\int_{-R}^{R}a^{-1}dt}=O(c),
\]
while the mean-zero \(L^2\) mass on the fixed-area thick pieces stays
positive. Min--max gives the claim. Tensoring those scalar functions with
any \(r_d\in\mathcal T_F\) yields actual four-dimensional TT near-zero
modes. They are not gauge artifacts.

The gauge operator itself has no corresponding degeneration:
\[
 \mathcal B\delta^*\alpha=\tfrac12\Delta_H\alpha+3\alpha
                                                               \tag{13}
\]
on the Einstein product. Thus its \(L^2\) gap is at least \(3\),
including harmonic one-forms. At the exact product, a Bianchi-compatible
forcing and the kernel-orthogonal solution retain Bianchi gauge by the
commuting identity \(\mathcal B P_0=(\frac12\Delta_H+3)\mathcal B\).

### Fixed genus still requires many retained modes above the cusp threshold

There is a stronger obstruction to keeping only a uniformly fixed number
of low modes. Pinch one simple geodesic on a **fixed** closed surface
\(B_c\), and keep \(F\) fixed. For every \(\sigma>3/8\),
\[
 \boxed{\#\{\text{positive eigenvalues of }P_0\text{ below }\sigma}
                   \longrightarrow\infty.}                 \tag{14}
\]

Indeed on one half of the embedded collar the radial scalar Laplacian is
\(-a^{-1}(a\partial_t)'\). The unitary substitution \(v=\sqrt a\,u\)
gives the **exact** Schrödinger operator
\[
 -\frac{d^2}{dt^2}+\frac34
               +\frac34\operatorname{sech}^2(\sqrt3t).     \tag{15}
\]
Choose \(3/4<\Lambda<2\sigma\) and a fixed \(T\) so large that
the potential on \([T,R_c]\) is at most \(3/4+\epsilon<\Lambda\).
The first \(N\) Dirichlet sine functions on this interval span a
space with Rayleigh quotient at most
\[
 \frac34+\epsilon+\frac{N^2\pi^2}{(R_c-T)^2}.
\]
Extend their corresponding radial functions by zero to \(B_c\);
these are legitimate \(H^1\) test functions. Taking
\(N\) proportional to \(R_c-T\asymp\log(1/c)\), min--max
gives at least \(N\) scalar eigenvalues below \(\Lambda\), counting
the constant mode at most once. The table then gives positive TT
eigenvalues below \(\sigma\), multiplied by \(6f-6\).

Thus no fixed-dimensional projection can preserve a uniform lower
bound greater than \(3/8\) on the remaining tensors throughout this
fixed-genus degeneration. This does not exclude a finite reduction at
a smaller positive threshold for fixed genus. If genus varies, even
the zero-mode dimension in (9) is unbounded. After removing those zero
modes, arbitrarily many additional eigenvalues can be made smaller than
any prescribed positive number by joining sufficiently many fixed
hyperbolic pieces with sufficiently short separating collars and using
the same capacity test on piecewise constants.

## 5. Uniform Sobolev control of the full complement

Let \(\Pi\) denote the orthogonal projection onto (6), and \(Q=I-\Pi\).
Use the geometric norm
\(\|h\|_{H^2}^2=\sum_{j=0}^2\|\nabla^jh\|_2^2\).
The complement inverse satisfies
\[
 \boxed{\|(P_0|_{\mathcal H})^{-1}F\|_{H^2}
                                  \le C\|F\|_2,}          \tag{16}
\]
with a universal constant. In particular the statement includes trace,
mixed, conformal-Killing, and all high-frequency sectors, not just one
TT projection.

Here is an injectivity-independent proof of the required global estimate.
The curvature action has operator norm at most \(3\), so
\[
 \|\nabla h\|_2^2\le2\langle P_0h,h\rangle+6\|h\|_2^2.
                                                               \tag{17}
\]
Since \(\nabla\operatorname{Rm}=0\), covariant integration by parts
and one derivative commutation give
\[
 \|\nabla^2h\|_2^2
 \le\|\nabla^*\nabla h\|_2^2+C\|\nabla h\|_2^2+C\|h\|_2^2.
                                                               \tag{18}
\]
The constant depends only on the dimension and the fixed curvature tensor.
Use \(\nabla^*\nabla h=2P_0h+2\mathring Rh\) in (18), and
use \(\langle P_0h,h\rangle\le\|P_0h\|_2\|h\|_2\) in (17).
This proves the uniform graph-norm equivalence
\[
 \|h\|_{H^2}\le C(\|P_0h\|_2+\|h\|_2).                 \tag{19}
\]
For \(h\in\mathcal H\), (7) supplies
\(\|h\|_2\le\frac13\|P_0h\|_2\), proving (16).
No coordinate ball or Sobolev embedding is used.

Because \(\Pi\) commutes with \(P_0\) and is an \(L^2\) projection,
(19) also proves that \(\Pi,Q\) are bounded on \(H^2\), with
universal constants. This remains true when the number of factor
deformation modes grows. Neither (16) nor (19) implies an
injectivity-independent \(C^{2,\alpha}\) inverse in dimension four.

## 6. The complement persists under actual small metric perturbations

This section concerns any smooth metric \(g\) on the **same product**
satisfying
\[
 \|g-g_0\|_{C^2(g_0)}\le\epsilon.
                                                               \tag{20}
\]
It need not be a product or Einstein and may have all tensor modes.
Define \(P_g=\frac12\Delta_{L,g}+3\); at a non-Einstein metric it
is still self-adjoint and equals the gauged first variation
\(D(\operatorname{Ric}+3g)_g+\delta_g^*\mathcal B_g\).

To compare Hilbert spaces exactly, write \(C=g_0^{-1}g\) and use the
\(L^2\)-unitary fiber identification
\[
 (Uh)(X,Y)=(\det C)^{-1/4}
                   h(C^{1/2}X,C^{1/2}Y).
                                                               \tag{21}
\]
It maps \(L^2(g_0)\) to \(L^2(g)\). Its coefficients and first two
derivatives differ from the identity by \(O(\epsilon)\).
Set \(\widetilde P=U^{-1}P_gU\).

Uniform tensor coefficient estimates give
\[
\begin{aligned}
 |\langle(\widetilde P-P_0)h,h\rangle_0|
   &\le C\epsilon(\|\nabla h\|_2^2+\|h\|_2^2),\\
 \|(\widetilde P-P_0)h\|_2
   &\le C\epsilon\|h\|_{H^2}.                              \tag{22}
\end{aligned}
\]
For the first estimate, compare the rough-gradient quadratic forms and
the algebraic Ricci/curvature terms in \(\Delta_L\). For the second,
compare the second-, first-, and zeroth-order covariant coefficients.
All needed changes are controlled by (20); no derivative of curvature
or noncollapse is required.

For \(h\in\mathcal H\), equations (7) and (17) show
\(\|\nabla h\|_2^2\le4\langle P_0h,h\rangle\).
For a universal \(\epsilon_0>0\), (22) therefore implies
\[
 \boxed{\langle\widetilde P h,h\rangle_0
       \ge\tfrac12\langle P_0h,h\rangle_0
       \ge\tfrac32\|h\|_2^2
       \quad(h\in\mathcal H,\ \epsilon\le\epsilon_0).}    \tag{23}
\]
This is a genuine uniform all-mode complement estimate on the transported
reference high space \(U\mathcal H\), not a claim about the moving
low eigenspaces of \(P_g\).

The compressed operator
\[
 A_{HH}=Q\widetilde P Q:
            H^2\cap\mathcal H\longrightarrow\mathcal H
\]
has a uniformly bounded inverse
\[
 \boxed{\|A_{HH}^{-1}F\|_{H^2}\le C\|F\|_2,
              \qquad \|A_{HH}^{-1}\|_{L^2\to L^2}\le2/3.} \tag{24}
\]
Indeed factor it by \(P_0|_{\mathcal H}\). Equations (16), (22),
and uniform \(H^2\) boundedness of \(Q\) show that the resulting
error on \(H^2\cap\mathcal H\) has norm \(O(\epsilon)\).
A Neumann series proves the first estimate and surjectivity; (23)
proves the second. This also identifies the operator-domain solution,
rather than stopping at a weak quadratic-form assertion.

## 7. The low equation is a full PDE with controlled high feedback

Let \(A_{LL},A_{LH},A_{HL},A_{HH}\) be the blocks of
\(\widetilde P\) under (6). For forcing \((F_L,F_H)\), the exact
linear elimination is
\[
\begin{aligned}
 h_H&=A_{HH}^{-1}(F_H-A_{HL}h_L),\\
 (A_{LL}-A_{LH}A_{HH}^{-1}A_{HL})h_L
      &=F_L-A_{LH}A_{HH}^{-1}F_H.                            \tag{25}
\end{aligned}
\]
The off-diagonal blocks vanish at the exact product and are
\(O(\epsilon):H^2\to L^2\). Consequently the Schur correction is
\(O(\epsilon^2):H^2\cap\mathcal L\to L^2\cap\mathcal L\),
whereas the direct low perturbation is \(O(\epsilon)\). The underlying
principal reference operator is (8), not a finite matrix.

Every mode eliminated in (25) feeds back into both the low operator and
the low forcing. Solving only its projection onto the constant
Teichmüller kernel is insufficient. Moreover, the uniform \(H^2\)
theory does not by itself supply a quadratic Einstein contraction:
four-dimensional \(H^2\) is not a uniform algebra controlling
\(C^2\), and the nonlinear equation and gauge have not been solved.

The global product hypothesis is also essential. A fixed integer-shear
manifold need not be diffeomorphic to \(B\times F\), and its collarwise
product appearances do not create the global projectors (6). The present
theorem supplies the complete model operator and a genuine neighborhood
complement theorem where a global product reference exists. To use it
on a sheared gluing one still needs compatible local projectors,
commutator estimates, actual transmission data, and the remaining low
PDE. Those are mathematical tasks, not assumed corollaries.

## 8. What this changes in the research problem

The product model has now been reduced without discarding tensor modes:

1. All scalar, gauge-generated, mixed, and complementary trace-free
   sectors have a uniform positive inverse, including small nonproduct
   perturbations in the stated geometric \(C^2\) neighborhood.
2. Every small denominator is carried by an explicit scalar Laplacian
   acting on factor Teichmüller coefficients. These are genuine TT
   fields, so gauge fixing does not remove the difficulty.
3. A uniform finite-dimensional replacement is not generally possible:
   long collars accumulate modes above the \(3/8\) tensor threshold,
   even with fixed topology; varying genus creates further arbitrarily
   small branches and unbounded exact kernel dimension.
4. The next equation is therefore an effective Teichmüller-valued PDE
   with the high Schur term retained, not another isolated waist
   coefficient or a blanket negative-curvature stability citation.

These conclusions specialize established product spectral geometry to
the precise degeneration relevant here. They do not settle the nonlinear
Einstein problem on the shear manifolds or a Yau26 sign branch.

`round17_product_operator_check.py` independently constructs the surface
connection and verifies (2), (5), the curvature block action, all spectral
shifts, the exact collar potential, and Schur algebra. Its ten groups
include rejection of omitted feedback. Global Hodge completeness,
min--max, and uniform perturbation bounds are analytic arguments above,
not consequences of finite program success.



---

## 原稿 13: round18_twisted_operator.md

来源：`mathematics/current/round18_twisted_operator.md`；范围：`full`。

原文件 SHA-256：`6a3be5e8f007dcf4b6c927a86c3e59d7dcaeac3ec766163533e5f0f7a72e7276`。

# Complete Einstein channels on finite-monodromy surface bundles

## 1. The geometric problem and the result

Let \(\widetilde B,F\) be connected closed oriented surfaces of curvature
\(-3\). Let a finite group \(G\) act on both by orientation-preserving
isometries, freely on \(\widetilde B\), and effectively on \(F\). The diagonal
action is free even when the fiber action has fixed points. Thus
\[
 M=(\widetilde B\times F)/G\longrightarrow B=\widetilde B/G,
 \qquad g_0=g_{\widetilde B}+g_F,\qquad \operatorname{Ric}_{g_0}=-3g_0       \tag{1}
\]
is an actual closed surface bundle with finite, possibly nontrivial,
monodromy. The notation for the metric in (1) means its descent to the
quotient. These exact Einstein metrics are classical; their existence is
not a new near-Einstein construction.

The result here is a full retained-operator description on this geometry.
Every symmetric two-tensor, not only a symmetry-preserving ansatz, is
included. The exceptional channels are Laplacians with explicit monodromy
and orbifold conditions; their orthogonal complement has a uniform inverse
even under small arbitrary metric perturbations. At each fixed quotient,
all infinitesimal Einstein deformations integrate, giving a complete local
nonlinear description and a residual-to-distance estimate. The latter
estimate is not asserted uniformly over degeneration.

The main conclusions are:

1. The low channels are flat Teichmüller coefficient bundles over \(B\)
   and the orbifold \(F/G\), not globally constant scalar coefficients.
2. The remaining full tensor operator has gap \(3\); its compressed
   \(L^2\)-to-\(H^2\) inverse remains uniformly bounded after sufficiently
   small \(C^2\) perturbations, independently of collapse and cover degree.
3. Finite monodromy can remove exact Teichmüller zero modes without removing
   arbitrarily small positive TT modes. The distinction is demonstrated on
   a specified genus-three hyperelliptic bundle.
4. Near a fixed quotient metric, every normalized Einstein metric is,
   modulo a small diffeomorphism, a quotient of deformed hyperbolic factors
   with the same actions. This is a local statement about all metrics on
   the quotient, not an assumption that a proposed correction preserves
   the displayed product structure.

The spectral inputs are the classical product identities, as in
Kröncke, [*On infinitesimal Einstein deformations*, Proposition 4.1 and
§4](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1508.00721). Their complete surface specialization
and uniform perturbation proof are in
[Round 17](round17_product_operator.md), §§2–6. The local deformation
argument is the integrable-kernel slice argument associated with Koiso's
[Einstein deformation theory](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/BF01393826);
we give the required integration and implicit-function argument below.
The contribution to the present project is the actual equivariant
transmission problem, its exact retained channels, and its degeneration
and nonlinear consequences, rather than a new classification of products.

## 2. Descent preserves the complete operator, not just selected modes

Use \(\delta h=-\nabla^ih_{ij}\),
\(\mathcal B h=\delta h+\frac12d\operatorname{tr}h\), and nonnegative
Laplacians. The full gauged normalized Einstein operator is
\[
 P_0=D(\operatorname{Ric}+3g)_{g_0}+\delta^*\mathcal B
     =\tfrac12\Delta_L+3
     =\tfrac12\nabla^*\nabla-\mathring R .                    \tag{2}
\]
Here \(\mathring Rg=\operatorname{Ric}\). Put
\[
 T_\Sigma=\{q\in C^\infty(S^2T^*\Sigma):\operatorname{tr}q=0,\ \delta q=0\}.
\]
This real vector space has dimension \(6\operatorname{genus}(\Sigma)-6\).
For the trace-free conformal-Killing operator
\(D_\Sigma\alpha=\delta^*\alpha+\frac12(\delta\alpha)g_\Sigma\), the surface
identities are
\[
 P_\Sigma|_{S^2_0}=D_\Sigma\delta,\qquad
 \delta D_\Sigma=\tfrac12\Delta_H+3,\qquad
 P_\Sigma(ug_\Sigma)=(\tfrac12\Delta u+3u)g_\Sigma.             \tag{3}
\]
All maps in (2)–(3) commute with isometries. So do the surface orthogonal
projections onto trace, \(T_\Sigma\), and \(\operatorname{ran}D_\Sigma\).

Lifting a tensor from \(M\) identifies it with a \(G\)-invariant tensor on
the product. This covers every tensor on \(M\). For every integer \(j\ge0\),
\[
 \|\nabla^jh\|_{L^2(\widetilde B\times F)}^2
      =|G|\|\nabla^jh\|_{L^2(M)}^2,                         \tag{4}
\]
while pointwise derivative norms are unchanged. The Reynolds projection
\(|G|^{-1}\sum_{\gamma\in G}\gamma^*\) is orthogonal and commutes with
\(P_0\) and all these factor projections.

For a scalar eigenvalue \(\lambda\), let \(E_\Sigma(\lambda)\) be the
scalar eigenspace, with its actual \(G\) representation. Write
\(H_\Sigma(\mu)\) for Hodge one-form eigenspaces, including harmonic forms
when \(\mu=0\). A superscript \(G\) in the following table means the
invariant part of the tensor-product representation, not a product of
separately invariant eigenspaces.

| Complete quotient sector | Eigenvalue of \(P_0\) |
| --- | --- |
| \((T_{\widetilde B}\otimes E_F(\nu))^G\) | \(\nu/2\) |
| \((E_{\widetilde B}(\lambda)\otimes T_F)^G\) | \(\lambda/2\) |
| \((E_{\widetilde B}(\lambda)\otimes E_F(\nu))^Gg_{\widetilde B}\), and the corresponding \(g_F\) copy | \(3+(\lambda+\nu)/2\) |
| \((D_{\widetilde B}H_{\widetilde B}(\mu)\otimes E_F(\nu))^G\) | \(3+(\mu+\nu)/2\) |
| \((E_{\widetilde B}(\lambda)\otimes D_FH_F(\xi))^G\) | \(3+(\lambda+\xi)/2\) |
| \((H_{\widetilde B}(\mu)\odot H_F(\xi))^G\) | \(3+(\mu+\xi)/2\) |

For orthonormal identifications in the fourth and fifth rows, use
\(D(\frac12\Delta_H+3)^{-1/2}\); for mixed tensors use
\(\alpha\odot\gamma=(\alpha\otimes\gamma+\gamma\otimes\alpha)/\sqrt2\).
The direct sum over the displayed eigenvalues is complete. Indeed the
product tensor bundle splits into the two pure blocks and the mixed block;
(3) splits each pure block. Restriction of this commuting orthogonal
decomposition to invariants preserves completeness.

In particular, mixed harmonic one-forms have eigenvalue \(3\), not zero.
The relative scaling mode also has eigenvalue \(3\). There are no missing
gauge zero modes: on the quotient the gauge operator is
\[
 \mathcal B\delta^*=\tfrac12\Delta_H+3\ge3.                 \tag{5}
\]

## 3. The retained operator as an actual transmission problem

The full low subspace is
\[
 \mathcal L_G=
 (T_{\widetilde B}\widehat\otimes L^2(F))^G
 \ \widehat\oplus\
 (L^2(\widetilde B)\widehat\otimes T_F)^G .                 \tag{6}
\]
Every tensor in (6) is TT. This is generally infinite dimensional, even
after the true kernel has been removed.

An isometry acts on \(T_\Sigma\) orthogonally for its \(L^2\) inner product.
Fix the coefficient representations by requiring that a coefficient map
\(u\) satisfy \(u(\gamma x)=\rho(\gamma)u(x)\), so that its associated
two-tensor is diagonally invariant. Using inverse pullbacks instead of
pushforwards gives precisely this convention. Define
\[
 \mathcal E_F=\widetilde B\times_G T_F\longrightarrow B,
 \qquad
 \mathcal E_B=(F\times T_{\widetilde B})/G\longrightarrow\mathcal O=F/G.
                                                               \tag{7}
\]
The first is an ordinary orthogonal flat bundle. The second is a flat
**orbifold** bundle if the fiber action has fixed points. Its sections are
equivariant smooth maps on \(F\); the underlying topological quotient
surface does not by itself specify their domain.

With the coefficient inner products induced by the factor \(L^2\) norms,
equations (4) and (6) identify the low operator exactly as
\[
 \boxed{P_0|_{\mathcal L_G}
   =\tfrac12\nabla^*\nabla_{\mathcal E_B}
        \oplus\tfrac12\nabla^*\nabla_{\mathcal E_F}.}       \tag{8}
\]
There is no curvature potential in these flat coefficient equations.

For example, cut the ordinary base along a system of loops. Across paired
faces, the coefficients and conormals satisfy
\[
 u_+=\rho(\gamma)u_-,\qquad
 \nabla_{n_+}u_+=-\rho(\gamma)\nabla_{n_-}u_- .             \tag{9}
\]
These are the full value and flux conditions; one cannot solve separate
Dirichlet problems and omit their matching. The minus sign uses the two
outward normals. At an orbifold point with stabilizer \(G_y\), the lift
obeys the full equivariance condition in a smooth disk. In particular
\(u(y)\in(T_{\widetilde B})^{G_y}\), and all jets obey the corresponding
equivariance. For an order-two rotation and a sign coefficient, the lift
is an odd function; its value vanishes at the fixed point, but merely
imposing value zero would not impose the correct smooth domain.

Integration by parts with (9), or equivalently on the finite cover, gives
\(\ker\nabla^*\nabla=\{\text{parallel sections}\}\). Thus
\[
 \boxed{\ker P_0=K=T_{\widetilde B}^{G}\oplus T_F^{G}.}     \tag{10}
\]
Let \(\lambda_*^B,\lambda_*^F\) denote the smallest positive eigenvalues
of the two coefficient Laplacians in (8). The eigenvalue \(3\) occurs in
the invariant constant trace sector. Hence the full optimal gap after
removing only \(K\) is
\[
 \gamma_G=\min\{3,\lambda_*^B/2,\lambda_*^F/2\}.           \tag{11}
\]
For a zero-rank coefficient bundle its entry is omitted. For each fixed
quotient this is positive. The low inverse is exactly twice the flat or
orbifold Laplacian inverse with parallel sections removed; the high inverse
is given by the positive shifted sectors in the table. This solves the
complete linear transmission problem, including its global constraints.

## 4. Uniform high-complement control survives the quotient and perturbation

Write \(\Pi\) for the projection onto (6), \(Q=I-\Pi\), and
\(\mathcal H_G=\operatorname{ran}Q\). The table gives
\[
 \langle P_0h,h\rangle\ge3\|h\|_2^2\quad(h\in\mathcal H_G),
 \qquad
 \|(P_0|_{\mathcal H_G})^{-1}f\|_{H^2}\le C\|f\|_2.       \tag{12}
\]
The constant is independent of injectivity radius, genera, diameter and
\(|G|\). For clarity, the uniform elliptic input is a global covariant
estimate, not an injectivity-radius assumption:
\[
 \|h\|_{H^2}\le C(\|P_0h\|_2+\|h\|_2).                  \tag{13}
\]
It follows by integration by parts from bounded parallel product
curvature, \(\nabla^*\nabla h=2P_0h+2\mathring Rh\), and one covariant
derivative commutation. Equation (4) cancels the cover degree on both
sides. Since \(\Pi\) commutes with \(P_0\), (13) also gives uniform
\(H^2\) bounds for \(\Pi,Q\).

Let \(g\) now be **any** smooth quotient metric with
\(\|g-g_0\|_{C^2(g_0)}\le\epsilon\), not necessarily locally product or
Einstein. Set \(P_g=\frac12\Delta_{L,g}+3\). For
\(C_0=g_0^{-1}g\), the unitary comparison is
\[
 (Uh)(X,Y)=(\det C_0)^{-1/4}
                 h(C_0^{1/2}X,C_0^{1/2}Y),\qquad
 \widetilde P_g=U^{-1}P_gU .                              \tag{14}
\]
On the cover this map is \(G\)-equivariant, because both metrics are.
The tensor connection and curvature formulas give
\[
 \|\widetilde P_g-P_0\|_{H^2\to L^2}\le C\epsilon,
\quad
 |\langle(\widetilde P_g-P_0)h,h\rangle|
      \le C\epsilon(\|\nabla h\|_2^2+\|h\|_2^2).         \tag{15}
\]
No derivatives of \(g\) beyond order two occur. The constants again
descend by (4). Since
\(\|\nabla h\|_2^2\le2\langle P_0h,h\rangle+6\|h\|_2^2\),
(12) and (15), with a universal sufficiently small \(\epsilon\), imply
\[
 \boxed{
 \langle Q\widetilde P_gQh,h\rangle\ge\tfrac32\|h\|_2^2,
 \qquad
 \|(Q\widetilde P_gQ)^{-1}f\|_{H^2}\le C\|f\|_2.}        \tag{16}
\]
The \(L^2\)-operator norm is at most \(2/3\). Surjectivity and the
\(H^2\) bound also follow by a Neumann series about the high inverse
in (12). It preserves invariants, so it is an actual operator on \(M\).

The low equation is not removed by this argument. For blocks
\(A=\Pi\widetilde P_g\Pi\), \(B=\Pi\widetilde P_gQ\),
\(C=Q\widetilde P_g\Pi\), \(D=Q\widetilde P_gQ\), it is exactly
\[
 (A-BD^{-1}C)u=f_L-BD^{-1}f_H,\qquad
 v=D^{-1}(f_H-Cu).                                        \tag{17}
\]
Here \(u\) is a section of both bundles in (7), with the actual matching
conditions (9), and \(v\) is the full high tensor response. The feedback
\(BD^{-1}C:H^2\cap\mathcal L_G\to L^2\cap\mathcal L_G\)
is \(O(\epsilon^2)\). It must nevertheless be retained if a twisted
Laplacian eigenvalue is of that order. Neither (16) nor (17) asserts an
injectivity-independent maximum-norm inverse.

## 5. An explicit nonproduct quotient and its exact channels

Choose a genus-two base \(B\) and a nonzero character
\(\chi:\pi_1(B)\to\mathbb Z_2\). Its connected unramified double cover
\(\widetilde B\) has genus three; let \(\tau\) be the deck involution.
Choose a genus-three hyperelliptic curve
\[
 F:\quad y^2=\prod_{j=1}^{8}(x-a_j),\qquad a_j\ne a_k\ (j\ne k),
 \qquad \iota(x,y)=(x,-y),                                \tag{18}
\]
with its unique curvature \(-3\) metric. Then
\[
 M_\chi=(\widetilde B\times F)/\langle(\tau,\iota)\rangle   \tag{19}
\]
is smooth, and has monodromy \(\iota^{\chi(\gamma)}\). The eight fixed
points of \(\iota\) do not create singularities in (19), because \(\tau\)
has no fixed points.

### The fiber representation is genuinely nontrivial

For a genus \(g\ge2\) hyperelliptic curve of degree \(2g+2\), holomorphic
quadratic differentials split explicitly as
\[
 \begin{array}{c|c|c}
 \iota\text{-parity}&\text{basis}&\text{complex dimension}\\\hline
 +&x^j\,dx^2/y^2,\quad 0\le j\le2g-2&2g-1\\
 -&x^j\,dx^2/y,\quad 0\le j\le g-3&g-2.
 \end{array}                                             \tag{20}
\]
At a branch point, set \(x-a=w^2\); the two forms behave as \(dw^2\)
and \(w\,dw^2\). At either point at infinity their orders are respectively
\(2g-2-j\) and \(g-3-j\). Thus all displayed forms are regular, are
independent, and exhaust dimension \(3g-3\). Taking real parts yields
\[
 \dim T_F^+=10,\qquad \dim T_F^-=2\qquad(g=3).             \tag{21}
\]
Using a genus-two hyperelliptic fiber would not demonstrate twisted
Teichmüller directions: its negative space in (20) is zero.

On the base cover, invariant TT tensors descend to the genus-two base.
Thus \(\dim T_{\widetilde B}^+=6\), and
\(\dim T_{\widetilde B}^-=6\). Write \(L_\chi\to B\) for the sign
flat line bundle. The two low operators in (8) become
\[
 \begin{split}
 &\tfrac12\Delta_B\text{ on ten trivial copies},\qquad
       \tfrac12\Delta_{L_\chi}\text{ on two sign copies};\\
 &\tfrac12\Delta_{F,+}\text{ on six even copies},\qquad
       \tfrac12\Delta_{F,-}\text{ on six odd copies}.
 \end{split}                                             \tag{22}
\]
The second line is equivalently over a sphere with eight cone points of
order two; \(\Delta_{F,\pm}\) denotes the Laplacian on smooth even or odd
lifts. The odd coefficients have no constants. Consequently
\[
 \dim K=10+6=16,
 \qquad\text{whereas the covering product has kernel dimension }24.
                                                               \tag{23}
\]

The topology confirms that this is not merely a product in disguise.
The involution acts by \(-1\) on \(H^1(F;\mathbb R)\), as seen from the
basis \(x^jdx/y\), \(0\le j\le2\). Finite-cover transfer gives
\[
 \chi(M_\chi)=8,\quad\tau(M_\chi)=0,\quad b_1=4,
 \quad b_2=14,\quad b_+=b_-=7.                            \tag{24}
\]
The signature is zero because it is zero on the degree-two product
cover. The quotient is aspherical. A product of two oriented surfaces
with Euler characteristic eight cannot have a torus factor; asphericity
excludes a sphere factor. Two factors of genera at least two would have
\(b_1\ge8\), contradicting (24). Thus (19) is not diffeomorphic to any
product of closed oriented surfaces.

## 6. Removing constants does not remove degeneration

For a fixed \(B\), the nontrivial line bundle \(L_\chi\) has no nonzero
parallel section, and hence \(\lambda_0(\Delta_{L_\chi})>0\). There is
nevertheless no uniform positive lower bound as the hyperbolic metric
degenerates.

Split the genus-two base along a separating curve into two punctured
tori. Choose \(\chi\) nontrivial on the first and trivial on the second.
It is trivial on the separating curve. Pinch that curve; an embedded
collar has metric
\[
 dt^2+a(t)^2d\phi^2,\quad a(t)=c\cosh(kt),\quad k=\sqrt3,
 \quad\phi\in\mathbb R/2\pi\mathbb Z,
 \quad R_c\asymp k^{-1}\log(1/c).                         \tag{25}
\]
The line bundle is trivial on the collar and the second thick piece.
Take a unit constant section on that piece, zero on the first, and the
energy-minimizing radial interpolation across the collar. Its energy is
\[
 \int|\nabla u|^2
       =\frac{2\pi}{\int_{-R_c}^{R_c}a(t)^{-1}dt}
       =2kc+o(c),\qquad \|u\|_2^2\ge c_0>0.              \tag{26}
\]
The piecewise smooth function is an admissible \(H^1\) section; smooth
approximations give the same bound. No subtraction of a constant section
is needed or possible in the nontrivial bundle. The Rayleigh principle
therefore gives
\[
 \boxed{\lambda_0(\Delta_{L_\chi})\le Cc\longrightarrow0.} \tag{27}
\]
By (22), this produces at least two actual positive TT eigenvalues of
the full four-dimensional operator tending to zero, even though the
corresponding constant fiber deformations did not descend. Twisting has
removed their kernel, not their near-kernel.

There is a complementary local effect when the character around a
pinched curve is nontrivial. Then
\(u(t,\phi+2\pi)=-u(t,\phi)\), so the angular frequencies are
\(n+\frac12\). For sections supported in a collar region where
\(a\le a_0\), angular Poincaré gives
\[
 \langle\tfrac12\Delta_{L_\chi}u,u\rangle
             \ge\frac1{8a_0^2}\|u\|_2^2.                 \tag{28}
\]
Thus sign holonomy eliminates the angular zero mode on that neck.
It is not a global spectral-gap theorem.

Both behaviors follow from the exact radial conjugation. For angular
frequency \(n+\theta\), \(\theta=0\) or \(1/2\), multiplication by
\(\sqrt a\) transforms the low operator into
\[
 \frac12\left[-\partial_t^2+\frac34+
       \frac34\operatorname{sech}^2(\sqrt3t)
                 +\frac{(n+\theta)^2}{a(t)^2}\right].     \tag{29}
\]
Where the collar holonomy is trivial, compactly supported radial sine
tests on \([T,R_c]\) give arbitrarily many low-channel eigenvalues below
any fixed \(\sigma>3/8\), as in Round 17. They remain legitimate
twisted-bundle test sections: the bundle is trivial on their support and
they vanish at the support boundary. If genus varies, the invariant
Teichmüller kernel can also grow without bound. Neither nontrivial finite
monodromy nor removing the kernel justifies a fixed-dimensional reduction
over all degenerating bundles.

## 7. Full nonlinear local closure at each fixed quotient

**Theorem.** Fix the metric \(g_0\) in (1) and \(0<\alpha<1\). There is
a \(C^{2,\alpha}\) neighborhood in which, modulo sufficiently small
diffeomorphisms, all metrics satisfying \(\operatorname{Ric}=-3g\) are
quotients of \(G\)-invariant hyperbolic deformations of the two factors.
The Einstein family in a local Bianchi slice is smooth of dimension
\(\dim T_{\widetilde B}^G+\dim T_F^G\). A residual-to-distance estimate
holds in a fixed Bianchi slice:
\[
 \inf_{\widehat g\in\mathscr E_0}
       \|g-\widehat g\|_{C^{2,\alpha}(g_0)}
       \le C(g_0,\alpha)
       \|\operatorname{Ric}_g+3g\|_{C^{0,\alpha}(g_0)}.    \tag{30}
\]
Here \(\mathscr E_0\) is that local Einstein family placed in the slice,
and the neighborhood and constant may depend on the fixed geometry.
The statement allows all tensor perturbations of the quotient metric.

**Proof: integration of the entire kernel.** A \(G\)-invariant TT tensor
on a surface corresponds to a \(G\)-invariant Beltrami differential.
Small linear combinations define nearby \(G\)-invariant complex
structures on the same smooth surface. Uniformizing each to curvature
\(-3\) gives a smooth family of hyperbolic metrics. Uniqueness of the
uniformized metric in its conformal class makes every fixed element of
\(G\) an isometry throughout the family. After the usual surface slice
adjustment, its derivative is the prescribed TT tensor. Taking the two
families together and then the quotient integrates every vector in (10),
simultaneously, in a family whose parameter dimension is \(\dim K\).
This argument uses the actual fixed actions, not only a numerical count
of invariant tangent vectors.

**Proof: there are no other local branches.** On the fixed smooth quotient
use the gauged map
\[
 \Phi(g)=\operatorname{Ric}_g+3g+\delta_g^*\mathcal B_{g_0}g,
 \qquad D\Phi_{g_0}=P_0.                                 \tag{31}
\]
The projection onto \(K\) is defined in \(L^2(g_0)\). Ellipticity and the
positive gap (11) give an isomorphism
\[
 P_0:K^\perp\cap C^{2,\alpha}
                   \longrightarrow K^\perp\cap C^{0,\alpha}            \tag{32}
\]
for this fixed metric. The implicit-function theorem uniquely solves
\((I-\Pi_K)\Phi(g_0+k+w)=0\) for \(w=w(k)\in K^\perp\) near zero.

The actual Einstein family just constructed can be put in the slice
\(\mathcal B_{g_0}g=0\) by small diffeomorphisms. Its derivative is \(K\),
because the kernel is TT and the gauge operator (5) is invertible. Its
projection onto the coordinate \(k\) is a local diffeomorphism. It must
therefore fill the whole graph \(g_0+k+w(k)\). Consequently its remaining
finite-dimensional obstruction map vanishes identically. There are no
additional nearby gauged solutions.

For completeness, gauged zeros are Einstein: applying \(\mathcal B_g\)
to (31), the contracted Bianchi identity gives
\(\mathcal B_g\delta_g^*\mathcal B_{g_0}g=0\). The one-form operator is
\(\frac12(\nabla_g^*\nabla_g-\operatorname{Ric}_g)\); it is positive
when \(\operatorname{Ric}_g<0\), as holds sufficiently close to \(g_0\).
Thus \(\mathcal B_{g_0}g=0\) and \(\operatorname{Ric}_g+3g=0\).
Every nearby Einstein metric can also be placed in this slice, so the
conclusion concerns all nearby Einstein metrics modulo diffeomorphism.

**Proof of (30).** Write an arbitrary nearby slice metric as
\(g=g_0+k+w\) and compare it to \(\widehat g=g_0+k+w(k)\). The derivative
of the complementary equation with respect to \(w\) remains an
isomorphism near the fixed origin. Its integral along the short segment
between these two tensors is also an isomorphism with a common local
bound, by a Neumann-series estimate about (32). Hence
\[
 \|w-w(k)\|_{2,\alpha}
       \le C\|(I-\Pi_K)\Phi(g)\|_{0,\alpha}.
\]
On the slice \(\Phi(g)=\operatorname{Ric}_g+3g\), proving (30).

One may also formulate this modulo diffeomorphisms by first taking the
local slice representative. This proof is the classical integrable-kernel
mechanism, with the complete kernel and its integration explicitly
established for the finite-monodromy geometry. It is not a claim that
all negative Einstein kernels in other geometries are integrable.

Equation (27) forces the \(L^2\) inverse norm of the transverse linear
problem to diverge at least as the inverse positive eigenvalue. The
theorem proves (30) only for each fixed metric; it supplies no
thin-parameter-uniform neighborhood or Hölder estimate. The uniform high
estimate (16) does not control these low directions or the nonlinear
Hölder constants.

## 8. What this establishes for the construction program

This closes a genuine linear and local nonlinear test case beyond the
trivial product: nontrivial monodromy, a specified nonproduct closed
four-manifold, complete transmission conditions, all tensor sectors, and
all nearby normalized Einstein metrics. It also isolates two independent
effects of twisting: elimination of some parallel deformation parameters
and holonomy-dependent neck coercivity. The former does not imply the
latter uniformly, as (27) shows.

The finite-monodromy condition is essential to this proof. An infinite
Dehn twist cannot act isometrically on a fixed closed hyperbolic fiber,
whose isometry group is finite. On a slowly varying \(E_D\) geometry,
fiber TT projections vary with the base; their covariant derivatives,
commutators with the Einstein operator, and actual seam matching must be
estimated. Equations (8), (12), and (17) do not by themselves provide
those estimates. They specify the full operator that a future adiabatic
extension must recover, including the low PDE and its high feedback.

The quotient in (19) already carries an exact negative Einstein metric,
so it is not a counterexample to Yau 26. Its role is a solved nontrivial
control geometry: a complete perturbative argument can succeed after
the monodromy and integrable kernel are handled, while thin parameters
still obstruct uniform inversion. This is materially different from
assuming an inverse for a new shear construction.

## 9. Reproduction

Run
```bash
/data/qiushi/qiushi_engine_v09/.venv/bin/python round18_twisted_operator_check.py
```
The independent script checks the representation projection, the full
sector shifts, local parity domains, hyperelliptic differential orders and
dimensions, the example's topology, cover-normalization cancellation,
the exact collar conjugation and capacity asymptotics, and the Schur
feedback identity with a nonzero-coupling negative control. These finite
checks do not substitute for the global decomposition, hyperbolic
uniformization, or the nonlinear implicit-function argument above.

来源：`mathematics/current/round20_construction.md`；范围：`full`。
